A.20 Executing Entity information


Therefore, it is expected that Component 1, EES would be ministries, water agencies, or other relevant government entities involved in water resources management and planning and hydro-climate monitoring and forecasting. On the other hand, EES for Component 2, EES would include national development banks, or other supervisory bodies responsible for infrastructure development, such as transport, energy, sanitation and solid waste management services, depending on the institutional context of each country. In some cases, state-level agencies (Secretariats) or municipalities/cities are often responsible for these functions.


Lastly, for Component 3, which focuses on strengthening transboundary cooperation at regional level, it envisaged that the Amazon Cooperation Treaty Organization (ACTO), as well as other EES with a similar mandate to promote transboundary water cooperation within the Basin under the signatory countries of the ACTO, would be responsible. In addition to the coordination of the riparian countries for the formulation and development of the Basin-wide strategy, ACTO will also play an important role in its implementation phase. In addition to ACTO and other EES, some activities within this Component could be IADB executed (given its competitive advantages for the execution of projects at regional level). However, it is expected that most activities within riparian countries via decentralized Country Offices, or the more appropriate executing entity, will be implemented by IADB. It should be noted that IADB and IDB will not jointly implement sub-projects.

1. **Climate change problem:** The Amazon region is highly exposed, vulnerable, and strongly affected by climate change. It has been impacted by extreme events such as warming temperatures and dryness, sea level rise, coastal erosion and ocean and lake acidification. The severity of droughts in some regions, with a concurrent decrease in water supply, thus impacting agricultural production, forest fires, food security, and biodiversity. The Amazon is the largest tropical humid forest with vast biodiversity, making it a global center of biological diversity (ACTO, 2024). It functions as a carbon sink, absorbing around one-third of all CO₂ emissions from human activities in South America and nearly 20% of the global discharge of all the freshwater that reaches the oceans (ACTO, 2024). The Amazon is also known for its rivers, which are often referred to as "flying rivers," which consists of part of the rainwater that falls in the region returning to the atmosphere and being transported by wind currents. These rivers transport around 20 billion tons of water vapor daily (FAO, 2004) from the Amazon rainforest to regions as far away as the Atlantic Ocean.

In 2018, the ACTO conducted the Regional Transboundary Diagnostic Analysis of the Amazon Basin. The analysis identified nine priority problems in the region, with water pollution ranking first, followed by deforestation and forest fires, soil erosion, desertification, land degradation, sedimentation, land use change, glacier loss, major infrastructure works, and insufficient integrated water resources management.


---
**Page 8**
---

3. Climate change significantly intensifies these issues, leading to severe and frequent occurrences with profound impacts on ecosystems, human health and well-being, and global economies. It is worth acknowledging that climate change is a multi-dimensional issue based on multiple stressors, exacerbated by rapid population growth in the Amazon basin since 2003 (Carrie et al., 2024).

4. Despite significant progress in regional and participatory planning, and institutional and public policies, degraded conditions in the Amazon Region continue to spread, while the effects of climate change slow and rapid onset events are increasingly observed. These changes have a direct impact on water availability, affecting the flow and maintenance of ecosystem services. In addition to socioeconomic vulnerabilities such as inequality, poverty, and lack of access to basic social services, these factors also affect the availability of resources for commodity production.

5. The Green Climate Fund (GCF) aims to improve water security conditions in the Amazon Region. This can be carried out by implementing specific actions to support water, sanitation, and waste (WSW) development projects that are climate-resilient and sustainable. These projects will contribute towards maintaining the maintenance and conservation of key ecosystem goods, and services for the provision of fit-for-purpose water, food, and energy. These actions contribute to GCF’s main goal, establishing adaptation measures in vulnerable communities and ecosystems, which is aligned with the long-term vision of the GCF in supporting developing countries in achieving their Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs) under the Paris Agreement.

6. Programmes will be designed to enhance the resilience of vulnerable communities, including Indigenous Peoples, and key ecosystems in the Amazon basin to the anticipated impacts of climate change. The programmes will focus on a water security approach that is based on an adaptation-focused and integrated water management approach that considers the current and projected changes in hydrological conditions (e.g., rainfall patterns, temperature, runoff) and the potential for restoration of the ecosystem’s capacity to provide essential hydrological services.

7. Climate adaptation benefits: The long-term benefits from Programme 2 will be(i) reduced physical and economic impacts of climate hazards such as droughts and floods; (ii) enhanced adaptive capacity of the population, infrastructure, ecosystems to climate extremes, with a focus on vulnerable communities including Indigenous Peoples; and (iii) institutional and regulatory frameworks will be enhanced to support climate-sensitive planning and development. The Programme will achieve these benefits through(i) strengthening the resilience of communities by implementing Ecosystem-based Adaptation (EbA), advanced technologies, and Early Warning Systems (EWS);(ii) increasing access to information on climate change impacts and empowerment of high-risk communities to enhance local resilience (Complementaries 1, 2, and 3).

8. Sub-programme 2.1 will focus on the development of a regional early warning system for water security conditions in the Amazon basin. It supports the achievement of the GCF’s fund level adaptation objective of increased climate-resilient sustainable development through activities focused on Climate Resilient Water Security (CRWS). The programme will support the implementation of integrated water resources management and integrated food production (IFM) works. About the planned results of GCF-2 for 2024–2027, the programme will achieve a regional early warning system in the Amazon basin through improved monitoring through new or improved early warning systems and, moreover, promote a regional EWS. This will assist six countries in the Amazon Basin to better manage water resources.

9. Furthermore, advances will be measured in four GCF Results Areas for adaptation: AR4 Most vulnerable people and communities, including Indigenous Peoples; health, well-being, food, and water security; AR3A Infrastructure and built environment; and AR4 Ecosystem and ecosystem services.

### B. PROJECT PROGRAMME INFORMATION

#### B.1. General Information (1000 words approximately 2 pages)

The Amazon Basin is characterized by high annual precipitation of 2,200 mm per year (the western region receives more rain than the southeastern region). The Amazon River generates 215,000 to 220,000 km³ of freshwater annually, forming enormous "flying rivers." This atmospheric humidity is a fundamental part of the South American monsoon system and plays an important role in climate regulation. The Amazon Basin covers around 5.3 million km², 40% of the global tropical forest area (Weng et al., 2018). Moreover, Amazonian ecosystems host between 10% and 15% of terrestrial biodiversity (Bubier et al., 2009) and store approximately 135 billion tons of carbon in their soils. The Amazon Basin plays a fundamental role in global biogeochemical and water cycles, making it an important buffer against climate change impacts on the planet.

The most common hydroclimatic events in the Amazon River Basin include amplified seasonal cycles, changes in precipitation patterns, and increased frequency of extreme weather events (Bouvet et al., 2022). These variations in precipitation translate into large alterations in downstream discharge (Sampson et al., 2018).

Climate change will aggravate this climate variability, potentially negatively impacting the Basin and the environment.

The Amazon region is at great risk due to climate variability and change, not only due to this phenomenon but also because of deforestation. The increase in wildfires represents an additional driver of change in the region (Chávez et al., 2019; Almeida and Sinclair, 2023). Furthermore, recent studies have highlighted the increased frequency of extreme floods and severe droughts in the Amazon Basin (Granato-Souza et al., 2022).

Additionally, it is essential to underscore that climate change disproportionately impacts women and children. The number of forest fires has been increasing rapidly in the last 50 years due to deforestation, which represents high risks for the region's biodiversity.

Climate change will lead to more frequent extreme weather events and decreased biodiversity (Wunderling et al., 2022). Climate change is expected to generate less rain during the dry season in the east and more rain during the rainy season in the west. This will have a significant impact on agriculture, especially in Central Brazil.

Figure 1 shows the temperature anomaly (°C) in the Amazon region for November 2023, when important weather events were observed, especially in Central Brazil, one of the most important areas in the agriculture sector (Meteobrasil, 2023).

### B.3 Problem statement

The Amazon region plays a crucial role in the global and regional hydrological cycles, affecting climate, precipitation patterns, and agricultural productivity across South America. Furthermore, it is home to one of the world's largest tropical forests, which provides critical ecosystem services.

However, climate change poses significant threats to the Amazon basin, with anticipated impacts that could disrupt these vital functions. These impacts are expected to be more severe in the Amazon region than elsewhere. Climate change includes an expected increase in the intensity, frequency, and duration of extreme weather events, such as floods and droughts, which are already evident across the region. These changes are anticipated to adversely impact water availability, biodiversity, ecosystem services, food security, livelihoods, infrastructure stability, and human health and safety of communities in forested and arid areas.

The impacts of climate change on the Amazon region include more frequent dry spells and heat waves, contributing to rising temperatures and extended drought periods. These changes affect agricultural productivity, water availability for domestic use, and energy production from hydropower plants. They also cause irreparable damage to ecosystems, raise the risk of extensive wildfires, and lead to increased CO2 emissions. The impacts are particularly severe in the Amazon region, including the northern part of central Amazonia, the delta region, and the border between Para and Mato Grosso in Brazil, as well as northern Bolivia, Peru, Ecuador, Colombia, and Venezuela.

The impacts on human health and safety include drought-prone vulnerable areas in Pura, Lambayeque, Lima, Ayacucho, Apurímac, Arequipa, Cusco, and Peru in Peru, as well as Oruro, Potosi, Santa Cruz, Chuquisaca, and Tarija in Bolivia. These impacts are expected to increase the risk of waterborne diseases, food insecurity, and loss of life.

The overall resilience, health, and well-being of the population and ecosystems will benefit from integrated approaches that address climate change at multiple scales, including local, regional, national, and international levels such as agriculture, food security, infrastructure, water, sanitation, and social protection. Such approaches must focus on adaptation and coordination at all levels of government to achieve long-term sustainability and adaptability (IPCC, 2022).

### Programme

The programme aims to enhance climate resilience and promote sustainable water management across the Amazon Basin. The underlying assumption is that IF supported activities such as deploying improved climate information services will be more effective in building the capacity of national and regional actors to catalyze transboundary cooperation are implemented.

This programme will contribute to achieving the following outputs:

1. Climate change adaptation strategies for the Amazon region, including the implementation of these activities will lead to better decision-making, improved water management, and stronger ecosystem services; these outcomes will contribute to long-term climate resilience by reducing vulnerability and increasing adaptive capacity in the Amazon Basin.
2. The programme provides directly to these measurable outputs, outcomes, co-benefits, and co-benefits, the Programme ensures that each step contributes to the larger impact of building climate resilience, protecting the Amazon Basin's vital ecosystem services.

For example, Activity 1.1.1 (Regional hot-spot analysis of the western region of the Amazon sedimentary aquifer system) will contribute directly to Output 1.2 (Climate Information and Early Warning Systems [CIEWS] enhanced), helping communities better anticipate and respond to floods and droughts.

---

31. These outputs in turn will lead to outcomes such as improved resilience of water systems and communities, reduction of climate-induced economic losses, and increased adaptation capacity for vulnerable populations, including indigenous Peoples. As a result, these outputs contribute to achieving the long-term impact of enhancing ecosystem services in the Amazon Basin, protecting biodiversity and reducing poverty.

32. The Programme will generate a range of co-benefits that further enhance its impact. These include:

- **Ecosystem Co-benefits:** By preserving ecosystems that support species in the Amazon Basin.
- **Socio-economic Co-benefits:** By increasing resilience to climate-related hazards such as floods and droughts, the Programme will reduce economic losses for communities, safeguarding livelihoods. Enhanced water security can also improve access to education and health services, particularly for women and girls, especially in rural areas, where improved access to water and sanitation services can lead to better living conditions.
- **Gender Co-benefits:** Targeted capacity-building initiatives will include a gender-sensitive approach, empowering women who are disproportionately affected by water-related challenges. By improving water access across the life cycle of women, the Programme will also empower girls from early childhood through menstruation age by enabling them to manage their menstrual hygiene effectively at home and at school.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 11

## Figure 4: Theory of Change Diagram

The figure outlines the objective of the Programme and several barriers have been identified:
- (i) limited data and ability to forecast or provide early warning on multi-hazard climate risks, combined with limited data exchange between basin countries.
- (ii) limited knowledge of climate change impacts on water security,
- (iii) poor implementation of existing water policies and regulations, including lack of consideration for climate change in water sector planning and considerations into sectoral water policies,
- (iv) lack of an enabling environment including regulatory incentives for adaptation investments in WSW sectors benefiting vulnerable populations, such as smallholder farmers.

The Programme aims to address these barriers by promoting climate adaptation actions that have a high potential to generate co-benefits that impact the population's well-being. These are four main co-benefits identified:
1) social improvements (improved access to water and sanitation services for women and children, improved carbon sinks)
2) economic benefits (increased agricultural productivity in vulnerable areas, increased income from climate-smart agriculture practices)
3) environmental benefits (reduced greenhouse gas emissions through improved land management practices)
4) gender and diversity (improved access to resources and opportunities for women and disadvantaged groups)

The Programme also aims to address the challenges inherent in managing multiple stakeholders and locations. Other risks were also evaluated, such as:
- Climate change adaptation actions promoted by the water sector have a high potential to generate co-benefits that impact the population's well-being.

B.3 Project programme description (max. 2000 words, approximately 5 pages)

The Programme has three outputs and will be achieved through five components, resulting in seven outputs that are structured as follows:

1. Component 1: Strengthening the knowledge base and information systems on climate change impacts to enhance preparedness and response to extreme climate and slow-onset events.

This component aims to build a comprehensive knowledge base and active information systems to improve weather and climate services for adaptation decision-making. By using advanced technologies such as real-time monitoring and dynamic decision-making tools, the component enhances access to critical climate data. This will enable stakeholders to make informed decisions that address both immediate and long-term climate adaptation needs.

2. In direct response to Barrier 1 and this component will bolster data management and develop decision support systems for short- and long-term adaptation planning. It will establish regional, transboundary protocols for shared hydro-meteorological and water-security data management and sharing of best practices in climate change adaptation. By supporting a collaborative transboundary water management framework to facilitate cross-border water resource management, this component will enhance the effectiveness of WRM activities. The component will also support Integrated Water Resources Management (IWRM) actions, addressing the challenges posed by climate change. Through the development and implementation of regional frameworks, this component aims to reduce community vulnerability, mitigate adaptation risks, and lay the groundwork for a collaborative transboundary (reduci) Management scheme that aligns water, land, and ecosystem management across basin boundaries.

3. Building on the component's emphasis on data sharing and governance, a transboundary water management framework will be developed through regional agreements, intergovernmental agreements, and cooperative frameworks for resource sharing among Amazon Basin countries. Collaborative platforms will be developed in partnership with regional entities such as the Amazon Cooperation Treaty Organization (ACTO).

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 13

## B

### Activity 1.1.1: Regional hot-spot analysis of the western region of the Amazon sedimentary aquifer system, including an aquifer vulnerability assessment to climate change and contamination from anthropogenic sources (priority areas would be identified during the project proposal stage). This activity will inform the development of a regional groundwater management plan that integrates water use and improve resilience. These studies aim to buffer the impacts of extreme climate events such as droughts and floods, while also enhancing the ability of communities to adapt to these changes in frequency and intensity of droughts and floods. This holistic approach will protect groundwater systems while providing a sustainable and adaptive response to climate impacts on water resources across the Amazon Basin.

#### Activity 1.1.2: Conduct studies to characterize the climatic impacts on river dynamics (including surface and subsurface flow, sediment transport, and nutrient loading) in priority areas of the Amazon River Basin (priority rivers would be identified during the project proposal stage). These studies will contribute to the development of a regional water management plan that integrates water use and improve resilience. The results from these studies will inform river dynamics, enhancing cross-sector collaboration in managing climate impacts. Studies will include gender and diversity considerations, ensuring that women’s voices are heard and their needs are incorporated into these studies, ensuring that water use and management are inclusive and support adaptation for all stakeholders involved.

#### Activity 1.1.3: Develop a centralized, regional information system linked with hydro-meteorological monitoring stations using real-time water-level data. This system will improve river flow forecasting accuracy and provide early warning of potential flooding or drought conditions in the region. The system will serve as a platform for regional transboundary protocols, enabling data sharing across Amazon Basin countries to support coordinated management of shared resources. The system will also monitor groundwater data where possible, creating a comprehensive view of the basin’s water resources for both local and regional stakeholders.

#### Activity 1.1.4: Strengthen water quality monitoring capabilities by combining global remote sensing datasets


---
**Page 18**

**(a). Sentinel MODIS, Landsat, RapidEye with continuous local monitoring networks. This AI-enabled system will provide detailed tracking of water quality changes, enabling swift responses to emerging pollution risks, improving early warning systems for floods and droughts. The proposed activity is part of the Integrated Water Multi-sector Nexus Management scheme by integrating water quality data with broader disaster risk reduction and climate data, facilitating sectoral collaboration. This activity will be financed by GF bid grant.**

**4.2.1.2. Development of a Hydrological Monitoring Network (HMN) for Amazon Basin countries. The Programme seeks to improve the basin-level hydro-meteorological network and make an Impact Based Multi-Hazard Early Warning System (IB-MHEWS) operational in the region, with a focus on early warning systems for floods and droughts, among other events, such as floods and droughts**. This will include integrating a new Amazon early warning center into the current Amazon Regional Observatory hosted by ACTO with integrated hydro-climate data provided by the proposed HMN. The IB-MHEWS will be based on an ensemble of models that can provide probabilistic forecasts for flash flooding events. It will include river routing capabilities to model hydrology connectivity among Amazon Basin countries, and it will also include a flood forecasting system. This new early warning system can be built upon the hydrofluv hydro-climate modeling platform in some riparian countries as an alternative to developing a new IB-MHEWS. The HMN will be developed by GF funded partners and will build on previous experiences of applying remote sensing monitoring where possible, such as Gravity Recovery and Climate Experiment (GRACE) (Toussaint et al., 2018). This activity will be financed by key ground hydrological monitoring stations.

**4. Output 1.2 will contribute to the understanding disaster risk through continued work on data ingestion, processing, and analysis for integrated water and drought management systems. The development of integrated water and drought management systems is a critical component in planning and management of Output 1. Interventions will align with Paradigm Shifting Pathways 1 and 2 of the Sendai Framework for Disaster Risk Reduction (UNDRR, 2015). This activity will contribute to the provision of hydro-meteorological services, the establishment of regional programs for data sharing and by making IB- MHEWS operational. The HMN will be developed in close collaboration with countries to ensure that it is well-informed, science-based decision making, helping to reduce loss of lives and damages to physical assets aligned with the Sendai Framework for Disaster Risk Reduction (UNDRR, 2015). ### Component 1:2.3. Disaggregate of gender & socially inclusive advisories that contemplate most vulnerable populations, including indigenous peoples to improve forecasting functionality. This will include socio-demographic characterization of the population, with disaggregation by gender and diverse groups (people who are elderly, people living in poverty, people with disabilities, etc.). The data will be collected through languages spoken in the areas of influence to use them in the dissemination of alerts and when applicable; community-based participatory monitoring programs. ### Component 1:4. Community based participatory monitoring programs, enabling trained local groups to collect and report data on water consumption patterns, water quality, and climate impacts. This data will feed into the early warning systems and enhance the overall effectiveness of the forecast and early warning systems. This activity will be financed by a GCF grant.

As these activities and outputs, Component 1 will generate the following outcome: Increased climate resilience in water resources management through enhancement preparedness and response to climate extreme events in the Amazon Basin (Outcome 1).

### Component 2: Catalyze investments for climate-resilient and low carbon water supply, sanitation, and waste (WSS) technologies and infrastructure. Component 2 will primarily address basins 2, 4, 5, and 6 to support the adaptation of WSS services in these basins. The Program will promote the adoption of Integrated Water Resources Management (IWRM) technologies, including Ecosystem-based Adaptation (EBA) interventions. This component aims to reduce water loss through demand-side management strategies such as metering, leak detection and repair, reuse, and strengthening protection from water-related disasters. Interventions will align with Pathways 1 and 2 of the GCF Strategic Plan, which are focused on climate-resilient and low-carbon WSS technologies and services. This component directly supports the adaptation benefits identified in the list of adaptation benefits for each basin (Outcome 3).

As part of enhancing water resilience, the Program will incorporate demand-side water conservation strategies such as metering, leak detection and repair, reuse, and strengthening protection from water-related disasters to reduce water loss and support real-time tracking in urban and rural settings. public education campaigns to foster behaviors such as household water conservation, agricultural, and industry, promoting long-term behavioral change (household, agriculture), and data-driven support, including a monitoring framework to measure water savings over time. Additionally, EBA strategies will be implemented through the restoration of degraded ecosystems, such as wetlands, mangroves, and riparian buffer creation, and reforestation aimed at natural flood control, sediment capture, and water quality improvement. This component will also support the development of a market for ecosystem services in rural communities and agriculture. This combination of demand-side, ecosystem-based and technological approaches will enhance resilience to climate change impacts on water resources.

This component has four main outputs that are strategically designed to ensure that the Programme can be adapted and adequately respond to specific needs of diverse populations, from small rural communities (Output 2.1), to large urban centers (Output 2.3). The Program will promote demand-side water conservation strategies to foster sustainable water use while focusing on ecosystem-based adaptation strategies, such as wetland restoration, mangrove protection, and reforestation aimed at natural flood control, sediment capture, and water quality improvement. This component will also support the development of a market for ecosystem services in rural communities and agriculture.

This output will create a financial foundation that underpins ongoing and future climate resilience activities, fostering a robust framework for adaptive management and scalable solutions. In parallel, Output 2.3 will promote demand-side water conservation strategies to foster sustainable water use while focusing on ecosystem-based adaptation strategies such as wetland restoration, mangrove protection, and reforestation aimed at natural flood control, sediment capture, and water quality improvement. This component will also support the development of a market for ecosystem services in rural communities and agriculture.

# Output 2.1: Sub-projects focused on community and ecosystem-based adaptation for small communities implemented

This output will target the most vulnerable small communities, including indigenous peoples, across all countries. It will prioritize projects that address water security (WASH - Water, Sanitation, Hygiene) infrastructure to cope with the intensification of climate change impacts such as droughts (leading to drinking water shortages) and floods (resulting in expanded flood zones, health risks, and loss of livelihoods). The projects will also focus on improving access to clean water and availability of traditional water resources, either because they are reduced or depleted, or because the capacity for their use is compromised. In addition, these projects will address the need for improved wastewater treatment before returning to the environment as necessary to avoid further contamination of water sources. Where possible, these projects should also consider the integration of climate change adaptation into existing WASH infrastructure.

These communities are highly interdependent in communities that historically had sufficient flow to dilute contaminants. Today, this is no longer the case and there has been a significant decline in fish productivity—the main source of protein in their diet—as well as diseases that affect development and health. These projects will also consider how climate change impacts water use and will be integrated into these projects to promote behavior change and increase community resilience, especially in high-risk areas.

The proportional distribution of sub-projects across target countries will be guided by the country’s vulnerability assessment (CVA) and the proportionality of the CVA. The baseline WASH context of each country and how climate change is exacerbating these challenges will also inform this decision-making process. This output will focus on addressing long-term water scarcity and economic pressures on communities. Without intervention, these issues are likely to increase in severity over time.

The selection criteria for sub-projects will be given to sub-projects that promote sustainable sanitation technologies, climate-sensitive waste management systems, rainwater harvesting (RWH) systems, improved irrigation techniques, managed aquifer recharge (MAR) methods, demand-side water conservation technologies such as smart meters and automated leak detection systems to monitor and minimize water loss; ii) monitoring water consumption patterns to assess impact of demand-side strategies over time for further improvement in water conservation; iii) the implementation of climate change adaptation measures under the GCF grant and loan, and ICB loan.

Sub-projects will implement community-driven water resilience projects specifically led by women and Indigenous groups. These projects will focus on critical water resilience efforts such as rainwater harvesting systems, improved irrigation techniques, managed aquifer recharge (MAR) methods, and automated leak detection systems to monitor and minimize water loss; ii) monitoring water consumption patterns to assess impact of demand-side strategies over time for further improvement in water conservation; iii) the implementation of climate change adaptation measures under the GCF grant and loan, and ICB loan.

Activities within this Output include:

* **Activity 2.1.1:** Implementation of Sub-Projects in Small Communities: Implementation of Sub-Projects in small communities will be selected based on a participative and inclusive approach. The selection process will ensure that eligibility criteria incorporate a strong focus on gender and diversity, taking into account the needs of women, youth, people with disabilities, and indigenous peoples, as well as persons with disabilities, and poverty levels. Beyond eligibility, it will be a requirement that gender and diversity considerations are reflected in project design and implementation. This will include ensuring a mandatory percentage of leadership positions within local water governance groups will be reserved for women and Indigenous groups to ensure adequate representation and ensure that the perspectives and needs of vulnerable populations are integrated into water management decisions.

---

activity will be on local ownership and ensure that adaptation measures are tailored to the specific needs and knowledge of these groups. This activity will be financed by a GCF loan.

* **Activity 2.1.3:** Development of a Customized “Amazonian Best Practices” Training Program. This programme will be for small communities in regional Amazonia, and it will focus on best practices for climate resilience and adaptation, tailored to the needs of small communities, particularly those living in vulnerable areas with limited access to resources and services, and those facing changing climate conditions. In these small communities, it will be advisable to strengthen the technical work with local organizations and institutions that have a strong track record of working on water management to ensure the proper use of sanitation facilities. This activity will be co-financed by GCF loan, and IDB loan.

* **Activity 2.1.4:** Development of a “Best Practices” Guide for Expanding Successful Interventions. This guide will provide lessons learned from successful interventions for expanding successful interventions, leveraging additional funding, and ensuring long-term sustainability of water and sanitation services in vulnerable communities. This activity will be financed by GCF loan.

* **Activity 2.1.5:** Development of a “Guidebook of Lessons Learned and Best Practices.” Develop and distribute comprehensive documentation sharing insights and best practices from implemented sub-programs to inform future initiatives and provide lessons learned for scaling up successful interventions in the region. This activity will include water management practices, highlighting the roles and contributions of women and marginalized groups in formulating and implementing solutions, as well as lessons on how to integrate gender equality into conservation efforts, ensuring that lessons learned reflect diverse perspectives and approaches to sustainable water management. This activity will be financed by GCF loan.

* **Activity 2.1.6:** Develop customized gender-sensitive capacity-building programs for women and marginalized communities on climate adaptation, water governance, and leadership. This will include providing resources for training and capacity building in the areas of climate change adaptation, water governance, and leadership. This activity will be financed by GCF loan.

### Output 2.2: Sub-projects focused on community and ecosystem-based adaptation for medium to large communities implemented.
This output aims to catalyze public sector investments in water and sanitation and other infrastructure that are critical for climate resilience in existing or planned national and municipal water, sanitation and waste infrastructure. Waste infrastructure improvements will include the installation of smart meters and automated leak detection systems to monitor and minimize water loss; construction of rainwater harvesting systems; and the development of stormwater systems, preventing blockages in drainage, and enhancing water quality. Activities will include climate-resilient sanitation systems and waste management solutions such as CO2 sequestration and flood mitigation. The main activities are:

* **Activity 2.2.1:** Implementation of sub-projects in medium to large communities, including project selection process and implementation. This activity includes the installation of smart water meters and automated leak detection systems to monitor and minimize water loss; construction of rainwater harvesting systems; and the development of stormwater systems over time for further improvement in water conservation efforts: ii) built solutions for water, sanitation and waste infrastructure improvements that are climate-resilient and contribute to reducing GHG emissions and waste sources. This activity will be co-financed by GCF grant and loan, and IDB loan.

### Output 2.3: Sub-projects supported by the EEA funds, to strengthen resilience of small vulnerable communities.
This Output includes the following activities:

* **Activity 2.3.1:** Analyze innovative financial mechanisms to generate EEA solutions, including identification of potential sources of funding and development of a business plan for implementation. This activity will be financed by GCF loan.

* **Activity 2.3.2:** Develop an EEA project proposal and monitor and track the impacts of proposed investments towards achieving climate resilience, GHG reduction, gender and social inclusion and diversity, and sustainable water management. This activity will be co-financed by GCF grant and loan to manage potential environmental and social risks of proposed interventions. This activity will be financed by GCF loan.

## Activity 2.3.4: Develop and implement promotion and dissemination plans to support national institutions (nIs) or NGOs to coordinate activities with other entities for direct investment in climate adaptation interventions, and to educate society on the economic value, along with its importance. This activity will be financed by IDB loan.

## Activity 2.3.5: Strengthen stakeholders working in related areas WSW projects, NBSs, etc., through actions that include capacity building, awareness raising, and knowledge sharing. This activity will be financed by the IFC associated with investments in businesses leveraged by ecosystem services and natural capital, and (ii) measures to support the development of a market for carbon credits and other financial instruments related to this mechanism. This activity will be financed by IDB loan.

## Activity 2.3.6: Provide incentives such as rebates for water-efficient appliances and subsidies for sustainable agricultural practices. These incentives will prioritize economically vulnerable populations, particularly women, youth, indigenous peoples, and Afro-descendant people. The use of technology is limited to foster greater accessibility and uptake. This activity will be financed by IDB loan.

### Output 2.4: Support provided for the origination, design, and deployment of adaptation measures in the WSW sector through (i) technical assistance to national institutions and NGOs to support the origination and implementation of climate-resilient water projects that integrate natural ecosystems into water management infrastructure; (ii) financial support to private sector entities to finance investments in climate-resilient water projects. EBA strategies will enhance flood control, improve water quality, and support biodiversity conservation while providing economic benefits for communities. The combination of public and private sector financing required through a weighted average interest rate, it will facilitate the deployment of investments, making them financially viable for the public sector, and attractive for private investors with weighted average interest rates that are competitive in comparison to other investment opportunities. This activity will be co-financed by GCF and IDB loan.

### Project Outputs

#### Output 2.4.1: Implement a “climate-resilient water program” for local water utilities to operate and maintain these systems, ensuring a sustainable foundation for long-term maintenance of new systems. This activity will be financed by GCF and IDB loan.

#### Activity 2.4.3: Climate resilient upgrades to infrastructure projects and processes involving incremental financing of climate-resilient infrastructure in planned projects in the WSW public sector. This activity will be financed by IDB loan.

#### Activity 2.4.4: Identify projects which incorporate large-scale investments on natural-based solutions (including protected areas, wetlands, and other ecosystems) to provide ecosystem services for flood control and aquifer recharge, and secure funding for their implementation through a strategic financial plan for public sector entities. This activity will be financed by GCF and IDB loan. The activities referred in Activity 2.3.1, allowing the possibility of leveraging resources from private investors in demonstrative projects that can be scaled up to other WSW projects. These investments will contribute to the creation of new jobs (including those related to the implementation of natural-based solutions contributing to the seed capital and/or investing in conservation activities). The nature-based solutions will provide protection against flooding, improve water quality, and support ecosystem services such as carbon sequestration; iii) protection and regeneration of aquifers through reforestation. All interventions under this Activity will contribute to the achievement of the project’s objectives by improving flood control and water management practices, ensuring continuous refinement and enhancement of EBA strategies for long-term resilience. This activity will be co-financed by GCF grant and loan, and IDB loan.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 19

## Activities E.4.4 Conducting training and capacity building of sectorial and finance public institutions of the riparian countries on climate finance mechanisms for climate adaptation projects.

This includes training in innovative financing instruments, such as blended finance, green bonds, and carbon markets, to enhance the capacity of public institutions to attract and manage both public and private financing. The training sessions will integrate a gender equality and diversity inclusion approach for development projects. By improving the understanding of the various types of climate adaptation projects and their associated costs, this activity will enable financing for climate-resilient infrastructure. This activity will build on the success of the ACROD Regional Action Plan (RAP) and the GCF-funded project “Enhancing Climate Change Adaptation in West Africa” financed by GCF loan.

### Output 49 Capacity building and enabling an environment for climate change planning and investment, regional exchange of data and information and transboundary cooperation mechanisms for coordinated implementation of adaptation actions among riparian countries

This output will support the adequate implementation of adaptation actions for WSV sector, addressing Barriers 3 and 4. This component develops a framework for coordination and collaboration on climate change adaptation and resilience enhancement among Amazon countries, including transboundary cooperation for water security, as follows:

#### Output 51 National and regional policies and institutional frameworks to foster an integrated approach to climate change adaptation and resilience enhancement

This output will support the development of national and regional policies and institutional frameworks that provide a conducive environment for climate change adaptation and resilience enhancement. This assistance will be provided to enhance national and regional policies and institutional frameworks to promote the integration of climate change adaptation into sectoral planning, including water resources management and disaster risk reduction. The enhancement will be defined through the adoption or revision of policies, regulations, and institutional frameworks that are in line with the WSV sector’s needs for climate change adaptation and resilience.

### Indicators

- Number of new or revised policies and frameworks that are enacted and promote climate resilience in the WSV sector.
- Formal transboundary cooperation legal agreements and mechanisms established between Amazon Basin countries, with protocols for data exchange, monitoring systems and resource management.
- Development of a regional framework for water resources management and disaster risk reduction and resource planning for enabling cross-border investments (i.e. Strategic Environmental Impact Assessment (SEIA) and environmental impact assessment (EIA)).
- Number of financial mechanisms that support long-term investments in climate adaptation, including leveraging local and international green finance for ecosystem-based adaptation EBA and Nbs.
- Capacity building on the use of gender-sensitive approaches to climate change adaptation (e.g. by gender) who can access and manage climate finance.

The components will be measured through the following indicators: The changes are 100% under the control of the AE and will be measurable through the following indicators:

#### Indicators

- Number of new or revised policies and frameworks adopted at the national, regional, and local levels.
- Number of agreements and cooperation protocols established for transboundary water management between Amazon Basin countries.
- Amount of green and climate finance mobilized for adaptation projects as a result of new financial mechanisms.
- Number of stakeholders (disaggregated by gender) trained in accessing and managing climate finance.

The baseline for this output will be established through a gap analysis (Activity 3.1.1), which will assess the current state of policies, institutional frameworks, and transboundary cooperation in the WSV sector within Amazon Basin. This analysis will identify existing gaps in transboundary cooperation, climate adaptation policies, and financial mechanisms that are needed to support the implementation of climate change adaptation projects.

#### Activity 3.1.1 Conduct a gap analysis of financial, institutional, and regulatory frameworks in each of the riparian countries for accessing and managing climate finance, including blended financing and international green financing for EBA projects in the WSV sector. The analysis will focus on identifying existing barriers and opportunities in each country’s policies and institutional frameworks to facilitate

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 20

## Investment in climate adaptation projects. Based on the results of this analysis, policy reform recommendations will be developed to enhance investments in climate adaptation interventions.

The recommendations will focus on strengthening the adaptive capacity and resilience of vulnerable communities facing high risks of droughts and floods, including indigenous peoples. The analysis will also integrate a gender-sensitive approach, ensuring that policy reforms promote gender equality and address the specific needs of women and marginalized groups.

### Activity 3.1.2 Implement a capacity building program at regional, national, and sub-national levels to operationalize the Amazon Regional Observatory (ARO) and other relevant initiatives. This activity will be financed by GCF grant.

#### Sub-activity 3.1.2.1 Assess existing data exchange mechanisms and protocols necessary to develop and implement an ARO platform that integrates climate change information with socio-economic, land use, and meteorological data. This platform will support early warning systems and regional planning for climate change adaptation in the Amazon basin. The ARO will be designed as a web-based system that provides water and weather conditions across the Amazon Basin, facilitating more effective management of floods, droughts, and pests. The ARO will also provide an open-source data portal to facilitate access to information and develop tools to ensure the interoperability of the Amazon Regional Observatory (ARO) with new hydrological and meteorological models.

#### Sub-activity 3.1.2.2 Develop a regional coordination platform or plan to track progress, facilitate knowledge exchange, and share best practices for climate-resilient and low-emissions investments in the WASH sector. This activity will be financed by GCF grant.

### Activity 3.1.3 Assess regional data exchange mechanisms and protocols necessary to develop and implement an ARO platform that integrates climate change information with socio-economic, land use, and meteorological data. This platform will support early warning systems and regional planning for climate change adaptation in the Amazon basin. The ARO will be designed as a web-based system that provides water and weather conditions across the Amazon Basin, facilitating more effective management of floods, droughts, and pests.

#### Sub-activity 3.1.3.1 Assess existing mechanisms for transboundary cooperation to improve resilient water management practices in the WASH sector. This activity will be financed by IDB grant.

### Activity 3.1.4 Assess existing mechanisms for transboundary cooperation to improve resilient water management practices in the WASH sector and identify opportunities for strengthening regional institutional frameworks (such as the ACTOR) and other bilateral/multilateral agreements. The goal is to identify, design, and implement a set of pilot projects that will demonstrate how improved resilience can be achieved through cooperation and coordination in the basin. Each proposed sub-project will clearly describe the adaptation benefits relevant to GCF result areas, including ARAC (Health, Well-being, Food, and Water Security) and ARAF (Water Resources Management). The project will also identify potential co-benefits from improved green infrastructure, WASH, Measurable GGIC indicators, such as the number of beneficiaries, improvements in water quality, and reduction in waterborne diseases during extreme climatic events. This activity will be used to track and report progress. This activity will be financed by IDB grant.

This approach directly contributes to Outcome 3: Enhanced governance and a supportive environment for fostering climate-resilient development in the Amazon basin.

### The proposed interventions are organized by adaptation strategy:

First, they aim to make water, sanitation, and hygiene systems climate resilient (CR-WASH) to manage future droughts. Second, they enhance integrated flood resilience management through improved early warning systems, better monitoring networks, and strengthening institutions. Third, they focus on improving flood resilience through integrated flood management (IFM), better monitoring networks, and early warning systems, promoting cross-border cooperation.

### Programme Eligibility Criteria:

The Programme seeks to support sub-projects that meet different eligibility criteria: (i) alignment with the GCF Strategic Plan 2018–2022; (ii) acceleration of climate action and support the implementation of NDCs; (iii) alignment with local policies and strategies, including those related to water resources management in the WASH sector and other sectors; (iv) contribution to improved transboundary water resource management and cooperation; and (v) inclusion of sub-projects that contribute to adaptation, mitigation, and/or both adaptation and mitigation. The programme aims to enhance the climate resilience of the Amazon basin's water sector by developing sub-projects centered around five strategic interventions, each targeting specific goals. Goals include enhancing

## B

### 8.4.5 Regional Programme Structure (RPS)

#### 8.4.5.1 Description of RPS

The RPS is a regional programme designed to strengthen the water sector's overall resilience. The sub-projects fall into several categories, such as NBS and EBA using green infrastructure, expanding coverage of WSS services through innovative financing mechanisms, improving water quality and quantity management systems, enhancing monitoring and preparedness, including EWS. Together, these initiatives aim to create a systemic approach to tackling climate challenges in the region.

#### 8.4.5.2 Programme Structure

The RPS will be structured as a Regional Facility, supervised by the IBRD as an Accredited Entity before the GCF. Once operational, through this facility, sub-projects that align with the Programme's objectives and deliverables will be implemented in each country. The programme will be designed to respond flexibly to needs and policies, with flexible schemes depending on the type of project and the financial instrument used. The RPS will be structured as a Regional Facility, supervised by the IBRD as an Accredited Entity before the GCF.

#### 8.4.5.3 Host Countries

The Programme will be implemented in Bolivia, Brazil, Colombia, Ecuador, Peru and Suriname (the "Host Countries"). The Funded Activity will be implemented through a new Host Country to the Funded Activity or other than these listed countries.

#### 8.4.5.4 Sub-Projects: Components 1, 2 and 3

The RPS will consist of a set of sub-projects approved by the GCF. These sub-projects will be implemented through a new Host Country to the Funded Activity or other than these listed countries.

#### 8.4.5.5 Activities under Components 1, 2 and 3

Activities under Components 1, 2 and 3 are based on the terms and conditions set out in the Term Sheet of the GCF. The RPS will be implemented through a new Host Country to the Funded Activity or other than these listed countries.

#### 8.4.5.6 Sub-Project Implementation

A Sub-Project will consist of a single activity or a set of activities under the respective Components ("Sub-Project"). A Sub-Project can be implemented by one or more Borrowers and/or Grantees, as appropriate to the terms and conditions of the FAA.

#### 8.4.5.7 Implementation Framework

The IBRD will be solely responsible for the transfer, administration, and use of GCF Proceeds for the financing of the Programmes. The IBRD will be solely responsible for the implementation of the Programme in accordance with the terms and conditions set out in the FAA.

#### 8.4.5.8 Sub-Project Management

The IBRD will manage all aspects of sub-project management, including project preparation, execution, monitoring, evaluation, and reporting practices, following the provisions outlined in the FAA. In this context, IBRD will provide management, monitoring, and supervisory mechanisms to maintain a transparent and effective administration of the Programme.

#### 8.4.5.9 Sub-Project Approval

The Borrower and/or Grantee will submit an application for approval of Sub-Projects. Following a formal request from a specific Host Country to the IBRD, a Sub-Project will be prepared, assessed, and approved as an individual IBRD project financed with GCF Proceeds.

#### 8.4.5.10 Project Preparation

The Borrower and/or Grantee will prepare a project proposal in accordance with the objective, scope and activities of the Programme as established in Section B.3.9 of this Funding Proposal and aligned to the terms and conditions set out in the FAA. The project preparation process is described in detail in Section E.5. All Sub-Projects shall also be included in IDB programming and strategic dialogue with each Host Country, in the context of local government priorities and existing collaboration with the IBRD.

#### 8.4.5.11 GCF Proceeds

GCF Proceeds could be leveraged with IDB's ordinary capital funds and/or other donors' resources combined to finance Sub-Projects.

#### 8.4.5.12 Due Diligence

The IBRD will carry out all appropriate due diligence on each Sub-Project, including all checks and assessments on the Borrowers and/or Grantees, and, if applicable, EEIs for identified EESs. The IDB will be a major shareholder in any EEI established by the Borrower and/or Grantee with the IBRD in accordance with the FAA.

It is the responsibility of the IBRD to ensure that EEIs monitor and collect the

## 1.2 Contractual and Implementation Arrangements

### SB Sub-Projects under the Programme will be executed through EEs.

For the financing and implementation of the Sub-Project, the Borrower/Grantee will enter into the following Subsidiary Agreements with the relevant borrower (Borrower) and/or grantee (Grantee), as applicable:

* **Sovereign Investment Loan Agreements** for Low Concessional Loans, and/or
* **International Financial Institution (IFI) Loan Agreements**, and/or
* **Other Financing Arrangements**

### 5.8 Guarantee Contract.

The Borrower shall be an eligible borrower (either the Host Country or public entities of the Host Countries) under the IIB policy. If the Host Country is not the Borrower of the Sovereign Investment Loan Agreement with the IIB, then the Borrower will enter into a Subsidiary Agreement with the IIB to provide a Guarantee Contract under which the Host Country, as the guarantor, agrees to jointly, severally and unconditionally guarantee the obligations of the Borrower under the Sovereign Investment Loan Agreement with respect to the GCF Reimbursement Funds. For example, if the Borrower in a Sovereign Investment Loan Agreement is a national development bank in the Host Country, then the IIB will enter into an agreement (i.e., a municipality or state) or a national development bank in the Host Country, in addition to the Sovereign Investment Loans between the Borrower and the IIB, the IIB will be the Guarantor Contract with the Host Country.

### 6.8 EEs.

In respect of each Sub-Project, it is expected that the Borrower and/or Grantee will be the EE for the Sub-Project in accordance with the IIB policy. If the Sub-Project involves activities where a Sub-Project needs to be carried out, in whole or in part, by a separate legal entity different from the Borrower/Grantee, in such a case, the IIB and the Borrower/Grantee will jointly establish an EE for the Sub-Project. The EE shall be responsible for the implementation of the Sub-Project if the technical execution of the Sub-Project is to be carried out in whole or in part by another legal entity. If the EE is established as a separate legal entity, it shall enter into an Execution Agreement with the Borrower/Grantee.

### 6.9 Option 2.

If option 1 is not feasible, the Subsidiary Agreement(s) set out above will be signed by the Borrower and/or Grantee and the third-party EE. The IIB will monitor and supervise that the third-party EE complies with its role as EE, and its obligations as such established therein. The IIB will monitor and supervise that the EE is executing the Sub-Project in accordance with the terms of the Subsidiary Agreement(s) set out above.

### 6.10 Option 3.

If option 2 is not feasible, the Subsidiary Agreement(s) set out above will be signed by the Borrower and/or Grantee and the third-party EE. The IIB will monitor and supervise that the third-party EE complies with its role as EE, and its obligations as such established therein. The IIB will monitor and supervise that the EE is executing the Sub-Project in accordance with the terms of the Subsidiary Agreement(s) set out above.

### 6.11 Option 4.

If the foregoing options are not feasible, on a case-by-case basis regarding the relevant Sub-Project, the IIB shall seek GCF’s instructions on the implementation arrangement for that Sub-Project.

### 6.12 In any event, under the terms of the Subsidiary Agreement, the Borrower/Grantee will be accountable for all obligations of the Borrower/Grantee and the third-party EE to be fulfilled by the satisfaction of the IIB. The Subsidiary Agreement will establish the remedies available to the IIB if the Sub-Project is not executed as required thereon.

### 6.13 Pursuant to IIB policy, this does not apply with respect to loans to development banks or agencies that have ample financial capacity to meet their obligations under the loan agreement and/or Subsidiary Agreement. This includes the provision that all the operations they enter into as borrowers are covered by a joint and several or subsidiary guarantee of the national government.

### Justification for GCF funding request (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)
Public sector financing to build climate change resilience for water, sanitation, and waste services in the Amazon Basin is limited to small or medium-sized grants and technical cooperation projects. This support understanding socioeconomic, hydrological, and institutional factors that influence the vulnerability of communities living on the periphery of these territories more vulnerable to the effects of droughts and floods, including indigenous people. Many of these communities are located in remote areas with poor infrastructure and limited access to services, making it difficult for beneficiaries who cannot afford them. Furthermore, projects with significant economic benefits but lacking financial viability struggle to obtain support from public or private investors.

The Amazon Basin is a vast region that spans nine countries, and the interventions designed for this basin have been limited to small or medium-sized grants and technical cooperation projects. resources over past two decades, resulting in a trend of reduced water surface. For Bolivia, Brazil, Colombia, Ecuador, Guyana, French Guinea, Peru, Suriname, and Venezuela, the interval between 2013 and 2021 was identified as the period with the lowest water surface in the historical series analyzed (MapBiomas, 2023). The combination of these factors has led to a significant reduction in water availability, increased competition for water resources, and the favor the depletion of forests by illegal activities, systematically depleting the strength of the Amazon river basin. This situation underscores the need for new financing formulas to respond to this challenge.

73. Development interventions need to consider additional costs to ensure systems are resilient to climate change. The proposed Programme will support investments in water infrastructure and services that improve the water resilience of local communities in the Amazon Basin. These interventions aim to improve the long-term adaptive capacities of riverine communities through more resilient systems with a higher degree of redundancy, which can better withstand shocks such as droughts, floods, and landslides. This includes losses due to floods and droughts, increased exposure to the risk of floods and thermal shocks, and the loss of livelihoods in rural areas.

74. Investments in traditional water infrastructure alone are insufficient for achieving climate resilience. The Green Climate Fund is crucial for funding the system's approach to technology, ecosystem-based adaptation, and governance. This includes investments in water infrastructure that can be integrated with adequate hydrometeorological services in a regional network necessary for the paradigm shift. The funding gap for these interventions is estimated at USD 10 billion per year over the next decade.

75. In the most vulnerable regions of the Amazon Basin, only a few financially viable interventions will be implemented in the lowest vulnerability and highest income regions, no investments are planned to be made.

76. GCF funding will not only target technology assistance and capacity building but also provide low concessional loans for infrastructure by de-risking projects, especially in low-income countries that have learned from adequate terms and interests. The Programme will also seek to cover over risks such as lower than expected guarantees, that other actors cannot assume. The Programme also seeks to invest in nature-based solutions and ecosystem-based adaptation (EBA) interventions, which are more cost-effective at a higher level of concessionality; the Programme would not be viable.

77. The Programme will support investments in water infrastructure and services, ensuring the effectiveness of public funds in delivering key water, sanitation, and waste services, ensuring climate-resilient infrastructure, including financing for Ecosystem-based Adaptation (EBA). Cost recovery in the water cycle is very low in rural and peri-urban areas of the Amazon Basin. This situation underscores the need for new financing formulas to respond to this challenge.

78. In conclusion, the proposed Programme addresses the urgent need for climate resilience in the Amazon Basin. Leveraging GCF funding, it aims to implement comprehensive, scalable solutions for systemic climate risks. The

### 8.6. Exit strategy (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

75 Successful implementation of Programme activities should translate into increased experience with EBA among the countries involved in EBA and NBI activities as well as EWS. It is important to mention that this is a demand-driven Programme which has been requested by the Amazon countries and will be implemented through their own institutions, including those established institutionally in the Amazon Basin. In its role to lead the implementation of the Amazon Cooperation Treaty (ACT), the GCF will support the development of an enabling environment for cross-border water management and technical coordination with civil society, scientific community, productive sectors and multilateral organizations allowing an effective multi-scale and cross-border stakeholder engagement for a successful Programme implementation.

80 The Programme will develop capabilities in ACTO and the Host Countries, at national and subnational level, encouraging sustainability through dissemination of lessons learned, technology transfer, among other activities. It will also support the development of regional water cooperation mechanisms and transboundary cooperation. The Programme implementation is expected to enhance sustained long-lasting cross-border water cooperation and improved water security investment planning at systemic and policy levels in the Amazon region. This will be achieved by building on the strong foundation laid in the dedicated efforts to strengthen institutional and technical capacities at regional and national levels for water governance.

81 The IDB will maintain its involvement in national sectoral policy dialogues and country offices, and its support provided through its extensive and expanding portfolio of technical cooperation and loan operations offered to the countries in the region. It is expected that the Programme will contribute significantly to the Amazon context with an increasing commitment of financial resources through the IDB’s Amazon Initiative and a strengthened focus on water security and climate change adaptation. The IDB will continue to support the sustainability of the enabling conditions catalyzed through this Programme. The Programme will be presented as a model for other initiatives in the region, including the recently launched Amazon Group in 2023 that aims at protecting biodiversity and accelerating sustainable development in three lines of action: (i) strengthening biodiversity conservation; (ii) promoting sustainable livelihoods in the eight Amazonian countries.

82 To conclude, this Programme already considers several mitigation measures for its long-term sustainability:

(i) strengthening of technical and institutional capacities of both the ACTO and national counterparts (technical and diplomatic offices), with specific focus on the development and implementation of innovative financing mechanisms (e.g., blended finance, public-private partnerships, knowledge/data exchange), and developing and/or strengthening the capacity of Accredited Agencies in the region to support the Programme’s activities. This will be achieved through technical assistance as well as those within IDBs Amazon Forever Program, including the GCF funded Amazon Bioeconomy Programme (ABP) and the Amazon Forest Carbon Partnership Facility (AFCPF). The ABP will also facilitate cross-sector technical cooperations and loans specifically in the Amazon Region; (iv) explore synergies with ongoing activities led by the IBD’s private branch (IDB Invest), other IBD-related initiatives and instruments (IDB Lab or IDB Green Fund); (v) ensure that the Programme is aligned with the GCF’s Strategic Plan 2021-2025 within the Programme, or other funds created by countries (i.e., Nature Smart Climate Solutions Fund or Amazon Forest Carbon Partnership Facility); (vi) explore opportunities for co-financing and/or partnerships with public sector institutions and private investors to expand financial resource mobilization for water investments in the beneficiary countries; (vii) link the preparation and implementation of the Programme with

*IDB financing may include IDB own resources or other funds administered by GCF on behalf of IDB from GCF resources. IDB loans financed with IDB own resources are subject to the approval of such funding for each individual Sub-Project. IDB loans financed with other funds administered by GCF on behalf of IDB from GCF resources are subject to the approval of such funding for each individual Sub-Project.

*Under IGF FFP Investment Loans started in 2018, the grace period is flexible as long as the Original Weighted Average Life (WAL) is not exceeded. Under IGF FFP Investment Loans started before 2018, the grace period is fixed at five years.

*SOFR-based interest rate comprised of SOFR + GCF Funding Margin + Variable DC Margin. The IDB’s Lending Rates are composed of i) SOFR + GCF Funding Margin + Variable DC Margin and ii) SOFR + GCF Funding Margin + Fixed DC Margin. GCF positions its lending rate on a quarterly basis, with the transition to SOFR, the periodicity of the calculation remains the same.

D. EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

The expected performance of the project/programme against the investment criteria as set out in the GCF's Initial Investment Framework.

### D.1. Impact potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

91. The core impact of this programme is the increase in climate resilience and water security in urban and rural communities in the Amazon basin. The programme is based on a system vision that incorporates several intrinsically related components to address complex water security issues and the water sector's adaptive capacity. It will also contribute to reducing vulnerability to extreme weather events, natural environmental influences on the participating communities' quality of life and health. It is estimated that the programme will increase resilience to the negative impacts of climate change and improve the lives of 10 million people.

92. This program's interventions benefit approximately 1.52 million inhabitants of the Amazon basin, of which around 78% are in rural areas (643,000) and 22% in urban areas (377,000). The programme will also have the interventions in the WSS sectors established in Component 2; and 1.16 million people directly benefited from climate change adaptation measures.

93. The programme will contribute to the adaptation of the GCF to achieve a more resilient sustainable climate that can easily revert to higher utilities in EBA and incorporate water-efficient technologies in all Amazon Basin countries. It will also contribute to achieving the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) on Health, Safety and Food Water Security and ARAPA Infrastructure and Built Environment and ARAPA Ecosystems and Ecological Services. For more detail see Annex 2.

### D.2. Paradigm shift potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

Baseline situation and shift in the development pathway: Climate resilient and low-carbon development pathways will be established through a series of strategies and plans, which will be achieved by enhancing technical capacities to support the development of projects and policies that promote climate resilience and water security. The programme will provide an integrated approach to data collection, models, and projections into a transboundary information system; the programme will achieve the following: (i) identification and prioritization of adaptation strategies and plans for urban and rural communities; (ii) it will provide a comprehensive understanding of hydrological dynamics in the Amazon Basin; (iii) identification and prioritization of adaptation strategies and plans for urban and rural communities; (iv) analysis and response to climate-related disasters; (v) integrated analysis of the exposure and vulnerability of socio-ecological systems along with both current and planned infrastructure will inform better risk management, planning, and decision-making processes. The programme will also provide a framework that enhances natural resources to extreme climate events will improve water cycle processes and increase community resilience.

Potential for scaling up and replication: The proposed programme is expected to have significant replication and scaling-up potential throughout the Amazon region by developing an all-for-purpose facility that can be used in other regions of the world. This will also contribute to improving access to water and sanitation. This potential to shift the trajectory of future investment in water, sanitation and waste management in the region will be achieved through a combination of technical assistance, capacity building in regional policy combined with knowledge materials will promote dissemination and adoption of appropriate management strategies and standards; (i) Development of climate conscious management.

Sustainability of the programme outcomes: The long-term sustainability of the programme considers reducing reliance on external funding by developing an all-for-purpose facility that can be used in other host countries, disseminating lessons learned, and achieving a successful knowledge transfer. See sections B6 and C3 for further details.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 33

## Contribution to knowledge and learning: This Programme will address knowledge sharing and learning on climate change adaptation interventions in water resources management at the hydrological system and its impacts on local communities (flooding, drought, water quality, etc.). Component 2 will focus on building capacity for the design, implementation, monitoring, evaluation, and the use of EIA interventions to address climate issues, but also on knowledge around how to sustainably fund climate adaptation interventions. The Programme will provide training at national level, regional level, and community levels around the regulatory environment to further enable investments.

## Gender co-benefits: Targeting gender-based initiatives will include a gender-sensitive approach, empowering women in decision-making processes. Additionally, it would reduce school dropout rates among girls and adolescents of reproductive age by enabling them to manage their menstrual hygiene effectively at home and at school.
