### D

#### 3.4. Needs and impacts (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

**103. The Amazon Basin faces significant impacts due to increasing droughts, floods, and heat waves, all driven by climate change. These effects generate substantial costs to ensure safety from flooding and water scarcity, which are not covered by the current budgetary allocations. This is a new challenge that was previously planned and budgeted for.**

**104. Despite this, there are socioeconomic conditions across the countries in this Programme, such as agriculture, livestock, and fishing, which are crucial for the livelihoods of many Amazonian inhabitants, are highly vulnerable to climate change impacts. These communities have limited access to resources that can help them adapt to climate variability.**

**105. The complexity and nature of the problem necessitates funding from public sources. However, current public budgets are inadequate because new hydrological conditions are surpassing the design parameters for infrastructure in the region. In addition, the existing capacity of the WSWF programme is insufficient to meet the increased demand. Adaptation to the adverse impacts of climate change and the need for long-term resilience measures are critical. The Programme will focus on building buffers and recharging for buffer climate impacts, are evident. This difficulty is particularly pronounced when funding natural solutions and ecosystem-based adaptation (EbA) in the Amazon Basin. The Programme will also focus on integrating climate resilience into ongoing activities and investments, demonstrating new sustainable practices for WSWF services scaling up across the basin, and catalyzing policies and guidelines for long-term resilience measures.**

**106. The strategic collaboration between GCF and IGB financing presents a unique opportunity to create synergies in funding and implementation of climate change adaptation projects that can be scaled up to inclusively address climate change and sustainable development. This partnership will leverage GCF’s resources and expertise, while IGB’s experience in scaling up WSWF services ensures that the Programme’s essential services can withstand and adapt to the evolving climate challenges.**

**107. The Programme is aligned with the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), with primary contributions to SDG 13 – Climate Action, SDG 6 – Clean Water and Sanitation, and SDG 5 – Gender Equality. Other secondary contributions include SDG 2 – Zero Hunger, which includes SDG 1 – No Poverty, SDG 5 – Gender Equality, and SDG 14 – Life on Land. Through these targeted interventions, the Programme will reduce poverty, improve environmental, economic, social, and gender co-benefits, strengthening climate resilience in the Amazon Basin.**

**108. SDG 13: Climate Action. The Programme prioritizes climate adaptation, directly addressing the impacts of climate change on water resources and ecosystems. By implementing early warning systems, ecosystem restoration, and early warning systems, the Programme will reduce climate hazards, including floods, droughts, heat waves, and landslides. These interventions will also contribute to SDG 14: Life below Water; broader goal of enhancing climate resilience in developing regions, aligning with international cooperation for sustainable development goals (SDGs).**

**109. SDG 6: Clean Water and Sanitation. By implementing water security measures, the Programme aims to improve access to clean water and sanitation services across the Amazon Basin. This will lead to improving sanitation infrastructure, enhancing water conservation practices, and supporting water quality monitoring systems. These interventions will reduce the risk of waterborne diseases, which can directly impact low-income, underserved populations. Access to reliable water and sanitation also alleviates the time burden on women and children, contributing to gender equity (SDG 5) and better educational outcomes for girls.**

**110. SDG 11: Sustainable Cities and Communities. Focused on resilience-building, the Programme will empower communities in vulnerable areas by providing them with climate-resilient infrastructure such as nature-based flood defenses and sustainable urban drainage systems—will reduce the social and economic impacts of climate change. These interventions will also contribute to SDG 2: Zero Hunger, which benefits over 1.5 million people directly and creates indirect benefits for millions more, including the protection of livelihoods and food security in vulnerable communities.**

**110. SDG 10: Reduced Inequalities. The Programme will reduce inequalities by prioritizing support for marginalized groups who are disproportionately affected by climate change. Access to climate-resilient infrastructure and resources will empower marginalized individuals, enhancing social inclusion and equitable access to water and sanitation services.**


---
**Page 39**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 35

These actions support the GCF's aim to foster inclusive adaptation measures across all population groups, particularly in underserved regions.

### Social and Economic Co-benefits
The Programme is expected to deliver substantial health co-benefits by reducing mortality from infectious diseases among children under five—a significant issue in low-income communities due to inadequate water and sanitation. Safe water access, coupled with improved sanitation infrastructure, will reduce the incidence of waterborne illnesses. This will allow resources to be redirected to other critical development areas, creating long-term social benefits for vulnerable populations.

### Environmental and GHG Mitigation Co-benefits
The Programme will achieve a mitigation impact over time by preserving and restoring forest cover in Brazil's Amazon region. These efforts will not only sequester carbon but also improve the resilience of ecosystems, enhancing biodiversity and local agriculture. These environmental benefits reinforce the Programme’s commitment to long-term climate resilience and sustainability, directly contributing to SDG 15—Life on Land.

### In sum
This Programme represents a holistic approach to sustainable development, enhancing climate change adaptation while protecting the Amazon basin while supporting the achievement of critical global climate and development goals.

| Brazil | The Brazilian Federal Constitution (1988) includes adaptation objectives: "incorporate adaptation measures into the actions of the National Water Agency," "Develop and implement a national strategy for water resources management," and "Promote policies to identify no-regrets" adaptation measures, targeted at enhancing capacity to adapt to the effects of climate change." The Brazilian Federal Constitution also provides for the main water sectoral populations, and ecosystems to foresee adverse effects. Brazil Country Programme (2013) includes a National Action Plan on Climate Change (NAPCC) and Country Programme development process and agreement of relevant national stakeholders. |
| Bolivia | Brazil presented its NDC to the UNFCCC on 09/11/2023 (4th update). It has adopted the National Policy on Climate Change (Law No. 1278/05) and its regulatory decree (Decree No. 736), it must include adaptation measures in all public policies, and it will be updated every five years with next updates. |

# Colombia

- **National Adaptation Plan (NAP):** Prepared by the Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development, February 2018
- **Law 1931 of 2018:** Signed into law by NDC to the UNFCCC on 30/12/2020 (2nd update)
- **National Climate Change Objectives:** Include the preparation of 135 River Basin Management Plans formulated and/or updated in accordance with the National Water Resources Policy, as well as the development of water resources planning and management instruments that contribute to reducing the risk and socioeconomic and environmental impacts associated with climate variability and change.
- **National Climate Change System (SISMC):** In progress

## Ecuador

- **National Adaptation Plan:** Prepared by the Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development, March 2023
- The priority in the National Adaptation to Climate Change Strategy is to manage the water heritage with a comprehensive and integrated approach by hydrographic unit to ensure the availability, sustainability, and quality of water resources for current and future generations.
- Ecuador presented its NDC to the UNFCCC on 29/05/2019: Adaptation objectives include:
  - Implementing a network of climate change in the water sector as a support tool for planning, management, monitoring and evaluation of effects of climate change on water resources and impacts on the socio-economic development of communities.
  - Incorporation of climate change criteria into National and sectoral policies, plans, programs, projects, and activities related to the water sector.
  - Climate change in the water sector: Incorporation of climate change criteria into National and sectoral policies, plans, programs, projects, and activities related to the water sector.

## Peru

- **National Adaptation Plan:** Prepared by the Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development, July 2021
- Peru presented its NDC to the UNFCCC on 3/12/2020: The National Water Risk Management System, which establishes a cyclical model composed of four main stages for analysis of the water sector in terms of climate change risk:
  - i) Identification and prioritization of adaptation measures;
  - ii) Development of a strategy for implementation of the adaptation measures;
  - iii) Monitoring and evaluation to measure progress in the implementation of adaptation measures.

## Suriname

- **Suriname National Adaptation Plan:** June 2020

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 59

## Replicability

Currently, there is limited knowledge of the potential impacts of climate change on water resources and ecosystems in the Amazon Basin. This proposal aims to incorporate planning for adaptation measures into existing development programs, including indigenous and traditional communities.

Institutions at both the national and subnational levels have the institutional knowledge and/or technical capacity to develop policies to address these vulnerabilities or consequences, such as water and sanitation management. However, there is a lack of coordination among factors such as the lack of evidence on climate change adaptation actions, reduces the potential for replicating lessons learned from the WGR sector.

## Sustainability

In its role of leading the implementation of the Amazon Cooperation Treaty signed by the five countries in 2015, the GCF will facilitate the effective participation of national and subnational levels. The proposal also facilitates the efforts to strengthen institutional and capacity building at national and subnational levels have the institutional knowledge and/or technical capacity to develop policies to address these vulnerabilities or consequences, such as water and sanitation management.

The monitoring systems, early warning and decision-making tools will be developed under Component 1 will improve knowledge of climate change impacts on communities, including indigenous peoples. The proposed programme will also incorporate a coordinated approach of EEA and EBA-based interventions under Component 2. These interventions are designed to assist with technical skills for the development of adaptation plans and water, sanitation and waste management projects.

Component 3 is designed to improve the sustainability of the investments and financial investment perspective; and raise awareness on climate change resilience among stakeholders in the Amazon Basin. The programme will also utilize lessons learned from other GCF-supported resilience projects (such as those under the Water, Sanitation and Hygiene Program), transferring best practices into the Amazon Basin to accelerate replication.

The programme strategy and its financing mechanism are designed to ensure financial sustainability by creating a strong business case for private sector capital for climate resilience investments. The proposed programme will also aim to attract and scale up the availability of capital.

## Core Indicator 6: To what extent does GCF invest in technology dissemination, demonstration and promotion of hydropower

The adoption of low-emission, cost-effective technologies and other innovative solutions is scarce and not fully harnessed in the basin.

For example, in terms of hydropower, the Atlas of Amazon Hydropower Networks of the World (AHA) and hydrological stations from the Brazilian meteorological network in five countries are listed in table 1 of Annex E (Feasibility Study).

## Core Indicator 7: Degree to which GCF investments contribute to market development and/or creation at the subnational level

The adoption of new technologies and innovations (such as NBS) and their dissemination with an impact on critical sectors within the basin are promoted.

In the target scenario, infrastructure, lack of financing, insufficient expertise hinder implementation, integration, and the development of Water, Sanitation, and Waste (WSW) solutions. The region's dispersed dispersed population make it difficult to access to services, financing, and enhanced institutional supportive regulatory environment across cross border cooperation and attracting private sector investment.

Components 1 and 2 of the Programme encourage the adoption of technology and innovation, whether on WSW or other critical sectors within the basin. These economic and environmental advantages offered by these actions serve to promote their replicability in the region.

The Programme will develop markets by improving regulations, enhancing data sharing, providing concessional financing, and promoting climate-resilient WSW solutions. Strengthened institutions and improved regulatory environment across the Amazon Basin by showcasing successful projects and models that can attract private sector investment towards sustainability.

## Activity 2.3.2 Develop a cross-cutting scorecard mechanism
- Develop a cross-cutting scorecard to assess, monitor and track the impacts of green climate funds towards security or resilience to hydro-meteorological hazards, food security and economic development.
- Define the effectiveness variables of the service by which interventions will be assessed in terms of security or resilience to hydro-meteorological hazards, food security and economic development.
- Define the control variables of the solution by which interventions will be assessed in terms of the inclusion of vulnerable communities, gender and social inclusion and strengthened environmental health.
- Build a dashboard with the aforementioned variables to monitor the resources needed to create the impacts of the service.

## Activity 2.3.3 Develop and implement dissemination plans to support national institutions and/or NDCs to coordinate activities with other actors for direct investment in climate adaptation interventions
- Adapt dissemination material of the new cross-cutting scorecard mechanism on climate adaptation to financial entities and the public.
- Coordinate activities with government agencies, civil society organizations and private sector entities in each country to enhance their capacity to engage in climate adaptation projects and gap closure using WSS systems.
- Coordinate with local or international development partners to identify opportunities for joint projects and gaps closure by WSS systems.
- Identify common interests and contact between stakeholders.
- Define specific promotion and communication activities.

- Define content to work with these entities to identify opportunities for joint projects, gap assessment, and opportunities in terms of ecosystem services and natural capital.
- Develop a list of companies and investments funded by GCF that can be used as learning and impact evaluation activities.
- Promote networking and coordination activities.

E

**E.7. Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words, approximately |1 page)**

Monitoring for the Programme will be in line with IDB’s and GCF’s policies and the terms of the FAA. The implementation of each sub-project will be managed by the project implementing agencies as per the provisions contained in the Loan and Grant Agreements between project implementing agencies and IDB; they are obliged to report on the use of proceeds of the Programme, the progress of the Programme indicators, and the environmental and social performance of the project to the IDB on an annual basis, in line with IDB’s standard reporting requirements.

Programme Level: IDB will prepare Programme-level Annual Performance Reports (APRs) in the format specified by GCF detailing (a) activities conducted during the year, status of implementation, potential issues, and solutions; (b) progress against targets and indicators given in this proposal. Sub-Project Level: A monitoring manual has been realised to provide a guideline on how to determine the impact of projects (See Annex 11).

Evaluation: A mid-term independent evaluation will be undertaken at year 3 and another one at year 4. And once the implementation period has ended, a final independent evaluation will be undertaken to evaluate programme results and impacts.

# F. RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## F.1 Risk factors and mitigations measures (max. 3 pages)

### Selected Risk Factor 1: Data exchange obstacles

#### Category
Technical and operational

#### Probability
Low

#### Impact
High

**Description**

Under Component I, the exchange of hydroclimatic data is crucial. Nevertheless, there is the possibility that the exchange will not be possible due to technical or operational issues. This could lead to a lack of accurate forecast information and reduce the effectiveness of preventive measures. Furthermore, the implementation of meteorological services in the Amazon region has been hindered by communication and cross-border barriers; however, these have been successful in the development of hydroclimatic monitoring for the Amazon River Basin (the area of high hydroclimatic vulnerability).

#### Mitigation Measure(s)

Efforts will be made to promote the exchange of information among the meteorological services of the Amazon countries. Work will also be done with the Brazilian National Institute of Meteorology (INMET) and other agencies to ensure that safeguards against third-party data breaches, along with other measures, are in place. The WMO/United Policy for International Earth System Data Exchange will be promoted. Coordination through ACTO on contractual data exchange protocol agreements will be fostered.

### Selected Risk Factor 2: Innovative technologies and EIA solutions not adopted

#### Category
Technical and operational

#### Probability
Medium

#### Impact
Medium

**Description**

A behavioral change does not occur in the implementation of the proposed Programme activities (including operations and monitoring) due to a lack of interest or willingness on behalf of the beneficiary organizations. This could be due to a lack of trust, unwillingness to invest in new technologies or maintain EIA interventions. There would also be a risk of not making visible the potential of EIA interventions.

#### Mitigation Measure(s)

Communities and influential actors at the termination of the Programme implementation will be involved in the design and operation of EIA interventions, which may be done through consultation mechanisms or forums. Financial instruments will be put into place to ensure that beneficiaries are willing to invest in new technologies and maintain EIA interventions. The participation of beneficiaries will be strongly promoted. Information will also be disseminated on successful examples of EIA interventions.

### Selected Risk Factor 3: Overlapping programs

#### Category
Technical and operational

#### Probability
Low

#### Impact
Medium

**Description**

Overlapping or mismatch between the proposed Programme activities and those being implemented by other departments or organizations in the Amazon area that duplicate efforts.

#### Mitigation Measure(s)

In the process of designing the proposed Programme, coordination has been maintained with the NGOs, the technical planning and finance ministries, as well as coordination through ACTO. This will help to avoid overlapping efforts within the framework of the Programme. The selection committee for projects to be financed by the Fund will exercise that response to the national investment and climate change plans. Within the selection process of projects to be financed by the Fund, the criteria established in the Project Selection Guidelines will be applied.

### Selected Risk Factor 4: Changes in staff or political leadership

#### Category
Probability

Impact

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Operational | Low | High |

### Description

There is a possibility that the EE’s capabilities to comply with the bank’s standards are, and they do not adapt to the requirements of the project.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

As per RBI policy, before the approval of each Sub-project, the EES will perform either an internal or external independent capacity assessment on the corresponding EES. This assessment includes overall technical capability, adequacy of resources, and other critical factors that may impact EE’s performance. The assessment also evaluates the EE’s capacity to meet market demand. The EE capacity assessment will include an analysis of the EE’s system to manage integrity risks, including its systems to perform due diligence on project developers and contractors. In addition, the EE will develop a plan to mitigate the impact of the identified risk. Furthermore, a market demand analysis was done by the EES to ensure that the EE is capable of meeting the project requirements in terms of capacity and technical market conditions. In addition, mitigating measures can improve EE’s compliance with bank’s standards. These include: 1) Conducting regular training sessions for EE staff on relevant policies and procedures; 2) Implementing robust monitoring systems allows to ongoing assessment of team performance and compliance with conditions; 3) Providing feedback to the EES on their performance, including the need for corrective action and management; 4) Improving the capacity of the EES by providing additional resources; 5) Conducting regular training sessions for EE staff on relevant policies and procedures.

## Selected Risk Factor: Medium

### Description

## Selected Risk Factor: Project pipeline development

### Category
#### Probability
Technical and operational | Low

#### Impact
Technical and operational | Low

### Description
There are risks associated with the project portfolio. The term "pipeline" is used to describe the progress of any activity toward a long-term goal that involves a series of discrete steps. For example, not assigning the appropriate priority to each task or having insufficient capacity associated with resources may not allow it to achieve one of the program's central objectives.

#### Mitigation Measures:
- **Effective communication and sharing clear objectives**: Clear objectives are ways to guarantee the correct development of the project portfolio.
- **Risk management plan**: This is a document that outlines how risks will be managed throughout the project lifecycle. It should include risk identification, assessment, response planning, monitoring, and reporting.

### Category
#### Probability
Governance | Medium

#### Impact
Governance | Medium

- **Decision-making mechanisms**: Ensure that decision-making processes are clear and transparent for all parties involved.

## Selected Risk Factor: Foreign exchange risks, rising inflation and interest volatility

### Category
#### Probability
Financial | Medium

#### Impact
Financial | Medium

### Description
IOF and IIB lending will be in USD, and loans to the final beneficiaries may be in local currency. Local currency volatility could lead to higher costs for the Program and the overall Program efficiency and effectiveness.

#### Mitigation Measures:
- **Currency conversion risk**: Consider the possibility of covering currency conversions for loans financed with offshore capital.
- **Interest rate risk**: The following steps are considered: a) Sensitivity analysis of how this would affect the Program; b) Sensitivity analysis to assess how changes in interest rates could affect the Program's cash flow and financial position; c) Conduct a stress analysis. Analyze to assess how changes in inflation would affect the Program; d) Perform sensitivity analysis on the impact of exchange rate fluctuations.
- **Inflation risk**: Also establish a plan to deal with extreme inflation scenarios: a) Identify critical areas of the program that are most affected by inflation and develop mitigation strategies for these areas. b) Implement more frequent procurement processes, c) Supplier diversification: find suppliers in different markets to reduce reliance on local markets.
- **Interest rate risk**: Consider the possibility of covering currency conversions for loans financed with offshore capital.

G. GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

### G.1 Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

#### Category B: Activities with potential limited adverse environmental and/or social risks that impacts individuals or communities are generally site-specific, largely irreversible, and not easily mitigated by standard and available mitigation measures.

This transaction is categorized as Category B, referring to the Fund's environmental and social safeguards-Division 8.07(2).

120. In 2010, the IDB adopted a new Environmental and Social Policy Framework (ESPF) that has ten environmental and social performance standards, including human rights, labor, community health and safety, indigenous peoples, cultural heritage, vulnerable groups, indigenous peoples, afro-descendants or other traditional groups. In addition to these standards, the ESPF also addresses issues related to pandemics with epidemics and are aligned with international best practices on biodiversity conservation, environmental protection, gender equality, stakeholder engagement, disaster and climate change risk and resilience, minimizing greenhouse gas emissions, and protecting the natural capital of the countries involved.

121. Sub-Projects to be implemented as part of this Programme will be designed, implemented, monitored, and evaluated in accordance with the ESPF's environmental requirements and international best practices. The procedures and guidelines also describe the process for assessing and managing environmental and social risks and impacts, as well as opportunities to improve the environmental and social outcomes of projects. The IDB is committed to ensuring that its projects are environmentally sound and socially responsible and promotes the adoption and implementation of these principles by its clients. Each project will require a detailed assessment of environmental and social risks and impacts.

122. Although the Programme is expected to have positive contributions to natural resources, it does not exempt it from potential adverse environmental or social negative impacts may occur. However, based on the proposed environmental and social management approach, these risks and impacts can be easily addressed through well-established mitigation measures and/or by avoiding them altogether.

123. The following table provides a general overview of environmental and social risks and impacts associated with the Programme and the corresponding mitigation measures:

| EAS Risks and Impacts | Mitigation Measures |
| --- | --- |
| All borrowers will prepare an ESPS that incorporates the ten environmental and social performance standards in their social framework, (i) identification of risks and impacts; (ii) assessment of risks and impacts; (iii) risk mitigation strategies; (iv) competency; (v) emergency preparedness and response; (vi) monitoring and reporting. | Implementing partners will screen sub-projects performance |

## EPSB Land Acquisition and Involuntary Resettlement

The acquisition of the proposed infrastructure might require land acquisition and/or involuntary resettlement of assets and access to assets and natural resources. The involuntary resettlement of population: physical displacement (loss of land, assets, or restrictions on land use), social disruption leading to loss of income sources or other means of livelihood.

## EPSB Biodiversity Conservation and Sustainable Management of Living Natural Resources

All subprojects must be designed and operated such that they do not result in the net loss of biodiversity in affected natural habitats.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 85

## Conversion of natural habitats, including surface waters
- Negative impacts to critical habitat areas such as wetlands and protected areas and intertidal areas such as KBA's and Ramsar sites.
- Terrestrial or aquatic: habitat degradation due to inadequate management of solid waste and wastewater collection, treatment, and disposal systems.
- Aquatic: habitat degradation is due to the conversion of surface waters, including short-term use of water for critical hydropower.

## Exclusion of sub-projects requiring significant conversion or degradation of natural habitats, including surface waters
Exclusion of sub-projects in highly protected areas that are subject to a high risk of cumulative impacts within their area of influence. The borrower should include a Biodiversity Impact Assessment (BIA) and provide biodiversity values for which critical habitats are designated.

## Sub-projects with significant adverse impacts on Indigenous Peoples will not be eligible
This includes relocation of Indigenous Peoples, loss of access to lands or resources, transfer of traditional ownership or customary use, significant impacts on natural resource-based livelihoods, significant cultural impacts, and impacts on Indigenous Peoples in isolation or initial contact.

Sub-projects in areas with presence of Indigenous Peoples, as defined in EPS 7.2 will carry out a sociocultural analysis to determine the potential adverse impact on Indigenous Peoples based on their social and cultural context.

In case non-significant adverse impacts are produced, mitigating measures shall be established and implemented to avoid or minimize them. If it is possible to compensate for these impacts, they should be commensurate with the nature and scale of such impacts and in the manner agreed by the Indigenous Peoples.

Additionally, sub-projects shall respect and foster full respect for the rights, aspirations, cultures, and natural resource-based livelihoods of Indigenous Peoples, including providing opportunities for Indigenous Peoples in a culturally appropriate way to participate in decisions affecting their culture, knowledge, traditional knowledge, and practices of Indigenous Peoples.

Throughout the sub-project's life cycle, an ongoing engagement with Indigenous Peoples will be established. The sub-project will be established and maintained based on meaningful participation by Indigenous Peoples in decision-making processes that affect them directly or indirectly and respect their rights. Free, Prior and Informed Consent (FPIC) of the affected Indigenous Peoples will be carried out when deemed necessary.

## Adverse impacts to indigenous and traditional peoples.
Sub-projects in areas with presence of Indigenous Peoples, as defined in EPS 7.2 will carry out a sociocultural analysis to determine the potential adverse impact on Indigenous Peoples based on their social and cultural context.

#### Gender equality

Sub-projects must assess and prevent adverse risks and impacts that may result from gender-based violence, including sexual abuse, exploitation and harassment. Sub-projects must develop and implement measures to prevent and address gender-based violence, including sexual abuse, exploitation and harassment in the community or other sub-project workers. These measures require:

- Developing, implementing, enforcing and monitoring a Code of Conduct establishing a zero-tolerance for SEAH against contractors, consultants and workers.
- All contractors, consultants and workers must adhere to this Code of Conduct.
- Establishing a grievance mechanism for reporting incidents of SEAH and to report violations of the Code of Conduct.
- Reporting all incidents of SEAH to the sub-project management through the sub-project’s grievance mechanism.
- Providing training on prevention and mitigation measures put in place by the sub-project against incidents of SEAH.

All sub-projects must put in place a grievance mechanism with specific procedures to receive, register and manage SEAH allegations.

#### Stakeholder engagement and information disclosure

Development and implementation of a Stakeholder Engagement Plan (SEP) is required. The SEP will outline the means for effective and inclusive engagement with stakeholders throughout the project lifecycle.
Discussion on environmental and social information in local languages and in an accessible and understandable format.
Consultation process with sub-project stakeholders, especially those affected populations, during sub-project preparation.
Establishment of a sub-project grievance mechanism for grievances related to the sub-project’s environmental and social impacts and management.

# G2. Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, emphasis: page 1)

### 123. Women are often considered as passive recipients of development projects, while various population groups that depend on and take advantage of natural resources have different roles and purposes in the management of these resources. The needs and interests of women (who are often seen primarily as users and managers of water resources) continue to be ignored or undervalued.

### 124. Amid the diversity of the Amazon region, women and men participate in different ways in making decisions about natural resource use and management. Women have a greater role in access control over them and derive benefits. These differences also generate inequalities in terms of vulnerability and resilience that affect both women and men in the face of environmental risks.

### 125. Additionally, climate change impacts on health for women and diverse populations in the Amazon region include indigenous communities. Rising temperatures during extreme weather events increase the incidence of maternal injuries, illnesses, and death. Women are also more vulnerable to water scarcity due to climate change, which puts diverse populations at increased risks of exploitation, human trafficking, and gender-based violence (UNHCR, 2020). In the face of these multifaceted challenges, it is imperative to empower these communities through inclusive decision-making processes, access to resources and design of sustainable livelihoods.

### 126. For this, a Gender Assessment was carried out to understand the gender situation in the course of the project implementation. The assessment identified several recommendations to promote gender equality, the empowerment of women and development with the inclusion of people living with disabilities (PLD), the inclusion of people from LGBTQ+ populations. With this purpose, the Gender Assessment, the Gender and Diversity Action Plan (See Annex 8) is designed, which provides a specific framework for gender equality and diversity in all aspects of project implementation.

### 127. These types of activities were included transversally in the programme. Component includes actions to promote women's participation in decision-making processes related to the collection and management of water, including Actions to prevent violence and support female entrepreneurship. Component 3 includes implementing six water management subprojects that will be implemented by women-led companies. In addition, a gender action plan was developed. In addition, a country study on local intermediaries will be conducted to establish a baseline of their support for women-led companies. Component 3 includes training actions, such as capacity building and awareness raising activities in the areas of gender equality and climate financing that includes a gender and diversity approach. The gender action plan includes specific measures to promote gender equality.

### 130. In addition to these planned activities, each subproject of the program will follow the IDEB's Environmental and Social Policy Framework (ESPF), which includes the Environmental and Social Impact Assessment (ESIA) for all projects. This assessment will identify environmental risks and impacts and introduce effective measures to avoid, prevent or mitigate them and thus, eliminate the possibility of creating gender inequalities or reinforcing pre-existing ones.

### G. Financial management and procurement (max. 900 words, approximately 1 page)

#### 131. The proposed Programme will be led by SSB Water and Sanitation Division (WSA) and the Amazon Coordination Unit (ACU)** in close coordination with the Climate Change (CCS) Division. WSA will lead the application of innovative synergies in the implementation of V projects by combining extensive know-how on the design and implementation of innovative financial instruments.

#### 132. Financial resources from the GCF will be managed in accordance with the AMA, with the establishment of the GCF Account by IDB. This account serves as the conduit for all GCF resources allocated to the Programme. The GCF Account is subject to the same safeguards and needs. IDB ensures commitment of GCF resources to specific sub-projects upon request.

#### 133. The GCF Account will be managed in accordance with the terms and conditions set forth by FAA requirements, outlined in Subsidiary Agreements. These agreements delineate eligible activities, disbursement methods, and implementation guidelines, including the structure of GCF-funded activities within WSA.

#### 134. Disbursements, reporting, monitoring, and evaluation of the Project adhere to IDB Policies and Procedures for Financial Management and Procurement (PFP) as well as the terms and conditions to IDB contingent upon approved resource allocations and effective Subsidiary Agreements in place.

#### 135. Following IDB policies, loan resources are disclosed to EEs as advances or reimbursable expenses. The GCF Account will be subject to the same requirements for financial statements and periodic reports, ensuring compliance with eligibility criteria and progress monitoring. The GCF Account is subject to the terms of the Subsidiary Agreement acting as EE and requires other EEs to follow applicable IDB procurement policies for GCF resources. The Bank’s Procurement Committee (PAC) or its equivalent (e.g., PACI, by acronym in Spanish) evaluates the key areas involved in implementation and execution. This evaluation plays a crucial role in assessing the capacity of the executing entity (EE). In addition to the Subsidiary Agreement, the GCF Account is subject to other arrangements. This supervision ensures adherence to procurement policies, with the Bank providing oversight for all procurements related to the GCF Account. The PAC or equivalent used to create a supervision plan that identifies the processes requiring prior review. Additional guidance on the use of international consultants and the capacity hiring international procurement consultants to assist the EE may be considered.

#### 137. The GCF Account undergoes external audits annually as mandated by the AMA, IDB subsidiary agreements, and other applicable laws and regulations. These audits ensure controls and the accuracy of financial statements for all trust funds, including the GCF Account. Fund resources cover audit costs.

Secretariat’s assessment of FP261

Proposal name: Improving Climate Resilience by Increasing Water Security in the Amazon Basin

Accredited entity: Inter-American Development Bank

Country(ies): Bolivia (Plurinational State of), Brazil, Colombia, Ecuador, Peru, Suriname

Programme size: Large

## Overall assessment of the Secretariat

The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

| Strengths | Points of caution |
| --- | --- |
| Strategic impact and alignment: The programme strongly aligns with the Inter-American Development Bank’s investment strategy, aiming to enhance climate resilience and water security for 1.52 million people in Bolivia and 300,000 beneficiaries across six countries, including vulnerable indigenous communities. | Variability in the capacity of executing agencies within countries may affect the quality and consistency of programme implementation. |
| Innovation and paradigm shift: The programme represents a significant shift in how climate resilience is addressed in the water sector. It introduces a blended finance facility designed to integrate climate resilience into water management. By creating a model that can be replicated, it aims to combine advanced climate information systems with practical adaptation measures on the ground. The goal is to achieve this by using a blended finance approach that merges ecosystem-based approaches with innovative financial strategies. | Success is contingent on sustained regional cooperation and alignment of national policies, which may be difficult to maintain over time. |
| Sustainability: This approach aims to develop long-term institutional solutions for climate-resilient, low-carbon water supply, sanitation, and wastewater infrastructure throughout the Amazon Basin. | The extensive monitoring and evaluation requirements across multiple countries and components could strain resources and expertise, potentially leading to gaps in data collection and impact assessment. |

Stakeholder engagement: The programme incorporates a gender-sensitive approach aimed at empowering women and marginalized groups, thereby enhancing


---
**Page 112**
---

### Summary of the Secretary's assessment

#### Programme background

The Amazon region faces acute vulnerability to climate change, experiencing significant impacts such as rising temperatures, increased aridity, sea level rise, coastal erosion, and acidification of water bodies. Recent severe droughts have diminished water availability, affecting agriculture, hydropower generation, and human health. Home to the world's largest tropical rainforest, the Amazon Basin stands as one of the Earth's most biologically diverse regions, supporting a vast array of flora and fauna, including unique vegetation and soil systems. Moreover, it constitutes the world's largest catchment basin, covering 4 per cent of the Earth's surface and discharging over 6,500 km³ of water annually. This represents approximately one-fifth of all river discharge into oceans globally and about 28 per cent of global freshwater output. The basin generates a unique "flying rivers" phenomenon, transporting sediment from the Amazonian interior to the Atlantic Ocean, which is critical for regions as far as northern Argentina.

According to the diagnostic analysis that the Amazon Cooperation Treaty Organization (ACTO) conducted in 2015, the following priority issues were identified: (i) water pollution, (ii) deforestation, (iii) biodiversity loss, (iv) extreme hydroclimatic events, (v) erosion, (vi) sediment transport and deposition, (vii) climate change impacts on agriculture and infrastructure projects, and (ix) inadequate integrated water resource management. Climate change is expected to exacerbate these issues, leading to more frequent and severe droughts and floods that profoundly affect ecosystems, human health and well-being, and the global economy.

Recently, for example, the Amazon River Basin has experienced an exceptional drought caused by persistently low rainfall and consistently high temperatures across the entire basin throughout the year.


---
**Page 113**
---

1. In response to ongoing regional and participatory planning initiatives, the Amazon Region continues to face escalating degradation, with both gradual and sudden climate-change impacts becoming increasingly evident. This degradation has triggered public health concerns and disrupted ecosystem services, leading to increased vulnerability of communities, particularly in areas where inequality and poverty are exacerbated by the region's economic dependence on natural resources. The resilience of these communities is therefore a critical element of the Amazon Region’s development imperative. This can be achieved through targeted investments in water supply, sanitation, and waste (WSW) infrastructure to strengthen climate resilience across the basin’s population, while improving access to clean drinking water, improved sanitation, enhanced livelihoods, food, and energy supplies.

2. The Inter-American Development Bank (IADB), has identified the following barriers that IDB will address with the proposed programme:

(a) Limited data and ability to forecast or provide early warning of multi-hazard climate risks, including those related to water security;

(b) Limited knowledge of climate change impacts on water security;

(c) Poor implementation of existing policies on climate-change adaptation for the water sector and limited integration of integrated water resources management (IWRM) and ecosystem-based approaches into national development strategies and policies.

(d) A lack of an enabling environment, including regulatory incentives for investing in climate-change adaptation strategies focused on water security;

(e) A lack of capacity for the design and implementation of institutional conditions for adaptation investments in WSW sectors benefiting vulnerable communities, including Indigenous Peoples;

(f) High perceived risks by the local financial sector regarding climate investments in WSW projects.

Against this backdrop, the proposed programme will strengthen the resilience of vulnerable communities, including Indigenous Peoples, and crucial ecosystems within the Amazon Basin against the anticipated impacts of climate change on water security and ecosystem services. The programme will do so through a multi-sectoral approach that includes an integrated water management strategy that accounts for current and future changes in water distribution and demand, as well as the conservation or restoration of ecosystems’ abilities to deliver essential hydrological services.

The total financing for this proposed programme is USD 391,428,631 with total GCF funding amounting to USD 275,000,000. The IDB will provide USD 116,428,631 and the World Bank will provide in senior loans with USD 57,276,213 as grants, which leverages USD 229,275,840 (USD 214,999,840 from IDB and USD 14,275,840 from the World Bank) to be provided by IIB. The environmental and social safeguards category of this programme is in B.

The programme will directly benefit 1,522,249 vulnerable people (3 per cent of the regional population), including Indigenous Peoples, and 6,751,701 vulnerable people (7 per cent of the regional population in the Amazon Basin area) within six countries.


### Component-by-component analysis

**Component 1:** Strengthening the knowledge base and information systems on climate change impacts and water security to enhance preparedness and response to extreme climate and low-stand events (total cost: USD 29,043.24 million; GCF cost: USD 27,133 million)


---
**Page 114**
---

## This component needs to bolster knowledge and access to information regarding the impacts of climate change on water security, thereby enhancing preparedness and response to both extreme and gradual climate events. It tackles bottlenecks of limited data availability and knowledge gaps in understanding the hydrological cycle, water quality, and ecosystem services and information systems, as well as developing decision-making tools to address short-term climate extremes and long-term trends affecting water resources.

This component is transformative and will complement IWBF efforts, which would then tackle the inter-basin transboundary issues. By addressing these knowledge gaps through improved water governance frameworks, this component aims to reduce community vulnerability, mitigate the risks of extreme events, and promote a more integrated and sustainable (IWRM) (water) management approach across basin boundaries. Specific activities include:

### Output 1.1: Amazon Basin-level water resource mapping and knowledge integration with national systems

- **Activity 1.1.1:** Regional hotspot analysis of the western region of the Amazon sedimentary aquifer system, including an aquifer vulnerability assessment to climate change impacts, focusing on arsenic contamination (arsenic) and anthropogenic (arsenic); surface-groundwater dynamic modelling for a better understanding of water resources in at least three (3) priority areas.

- **Activity 1.1.2:** Conduct studies to characterise the climate impacts on river dynamics and surface and groundwater interactions by combining hydrological and hydraulic modelling of priority Amazonian rivers (prioritised according to their importance for human activities).

- **Activity 1.1.3:** Support of a regional water resources information system, through water-balance modelling, linked to hydrometeorological monitoring stations and other data sources, with the aim of developing capabilities for incorporating climate change and water demand scenarios that include groundwater.

- **Activity 1.1.4:** Support water-quality monitoring systems by combining international remote sensing data sets (i.e., Sentinel, Modis, Landsat, RapidEye) and local in-situ measurements to improve water quality assessment.

### Output 1.2: Climate information and early warning systems enhanced

- **Activity 1.2.1:** Advance priority monitoring stations for an integrated flood and drought management system, including the establishment of about 35 additional stations and 4 Doppler radar units; 

- **Activity 1.2.2:** Development of a data-processing centre for the Amazon Basin, integrating climate information systems with early warning systems across, including promoting basin-level models such as the Flash Flood Guidance System (Flood Group in Earth Observation Global Water Sustainability).

- **Activity 1.2.3:** Develop and disseminate climate change and water advisories that consider most vulnerable populations, including Indigenous Peoples to improve forecasting functionality.


---
**Page 115**
---

### Component 2: Catalyst climate investments for climate-resilient and low-carbon water supply, sanitation, and waste technologies and infrastructure (Total cost USD 133.72 million; GCF cost USD 116.72 million)

#### 15. Component 2 will focus on overcoming barriers around 1) limited knowledge; 2) limited enabling environment; and 3) lack of concessional and patient capital, to support climate-resilient and low-carbon WSTS technologies and infrastructure in the Amazon Basin region. This is a multi-year initiative that aims to scale up the adoption of innovative water sector solutions, including Ebas technologies, aiming at Ebas strategies. The goal is to reduce the water sector's vulnerability to climate change by increasing access to safe drinking water, sanitation services, and bolstering protection against water-related disasters. These interventions are aligned with pathways 1 and 2 of the GCF Water Security Guideline which emphasizes preserving water resources while ensuring equitable access to water for all.

#### 16. The component supports various adaptation benefits, ensuring communities better equipped to handle climate change impacts.

The component includes four main output designs aimed at adapting to the specific needs of various populations including small rural communities (output 2.1 large), more complex cities (output 2.2), and larger urban areas (output 2.3). The overall goal is to ensure that each project intervention is designed in a way that maximizes its impact, promotes resilience and adaptation, and creates new financial mechanisms and funds to cover operational costs, support disaster recovery efforts, and enhance the long-term sustainability of interventions.

This output will establish a financial foundation for ongoing and future investments by developing innovative financing models, adaptive management and scalable solutions. Specific activities under this component include:

(a) Output 2.1: Subjects focused on community and Ebas for small communities implementing:
(i) Activity 2.2.1: Implementation of subprojects in small communities;
(ii) Activity 2.2.2: Design and implement community-driven water-resilience projects specifically led by women and Indigenous groups;
(iii) Activity 2.2.3: Development of a customized "Amazonian best practices" training programme;
(iv) Activity 2.2.4: Development of a strategy for scaling up investments;
(v) Activity 2.2.5: Recognition of local knowledge and traditional practices;
(vi) Activity 2.2.6: Develop customized gender-sensitive capacity-building programmes for women and marginalized communities on climate adaptation, water security, and livelihoods.

(b) Output 2.2: Subjects focused on community and Ebas for medium to large communities implementing:
(i) Activity 2.2.1: Implementation of subprojects in medium to large communities, including implementation using eligibility criteria and stakeholder consultations;
(ii) Output 2.3: Technologies and small-scale adaptation measures on the WSW sectors supported, aimed at strengthening resilience of small vulnerable communities:
(iii) Activity 2.3.1: Analyse innovative financial mechanisms to generate Ebas solutions; networking, identification, design and proposal of specific technical and


---
**Page 116**
---

Financial mechanisms to promote private-public participation for financing the implementation of Ebba (thematic bonds, water funds, parametric insurance, performance-based contracts, guarantees):

- **Activity 2.3.2:** Develop a cross-cutting scorecard mechanism for Ebba, monitor and evaluate progress towards climate resilience, greenhouse gas reduction, gender and social inclusion and strengthen institutional capacity to implement Ebba.

- **Activity 2.3.3:** Develop and implement promotion and dissemination plans to support national institutions and/or national development banks to coordinate activities with the private sector, including through blended finance interventions and to educate society on their economic value and importance;

- **Activity 2.3.4:** Strengthen stakeholders working in related areas, WSW sectors, natural resources management, water resource planning, and climate change resource allocation decisions; (ii) correctly assessing risk and opportunities associated with the use of Ebba funds for investments in water services and natural capital; and (iii) measuring and monitoring environmental and social impacts of Ebba projects.

- **Activity 2.3.5:** Design a programme offering incentives, such as rebates for water-efficient appliances and subsidies for sustainable agricultural practices;

- **Output 2.4:** Support provided for the origination, design, and deployment of adaptation measures in the WSW public sector, emphasizing:

    - Activity 2.4.1: Develop a strategic plan of WSW infrastructure projects in the region to develop and implement a strategic investment plan aiming to:
        * Upgrade existing water, sanitation and solid waste infrastructure;
        * Prioritize activities that enhance climate resilience in ongoing projects;
        * Identify opportunities and develop a strategic financing plan for future climate-sensitive drinking water and sanitation investments in the Amazon;
        * Identify cultural adaptation needs for diverse population groups; and
        * Conduct skills gap assessments to identify areas where local workers and institutions need training.

    - Activity 2.4.2: Establish a 'Train-the-trainer' programme for local water utilities to operate and maintain these systems, ensuring a sustainable foundation for long-term climate resilience;

- **Activity 2.4.3:** Climate-resilient upgrades to infrastructure projects and investments in the WSW public sector; and (iv) promote resilient infrastructure in planned projects in the WSW public sector.

- **Activity 2.4.4:** Identify projects which incorporate large-scale investments on natural resources management, water resource planning, and climate change resilience in priority areas for drought management, flood control and aquifer recharge, and secure funding for their implementation through a strategic financial plan for public utilities.

# Cooperation mechanisms for water security (Total cost: USD 98 million; GCF cost: USD 4.5 million)

## Technical assistance aimed at guiding the development and effective implementation of adaptation activities within the WSW sector

### Output 3.1.1: National and regional policies and institutional frameworks to foster an integrated approach to adaptation looking at the basin as a transboundary system enhanced

#### Activity 3.1.1.1: Conduct a gap analysis of financial, institutional, and regulatory frameworks in each of the countries benefiting from the programme to assess water security needs and identify gaps of local and international green financing for EBA projects in the WSW sector.

#### Activity 3.1.1.2: Implement a capacity-building programme at regional, national, and subnational levels to optimize access to grants and climate financing for WSW projects.

#### Activity 3.1.1.3: Develop and maintain an Amazon hydroclimatic information platform for the access and exchange of hydrological and meteorological data; and

#### Activity 3.1.1.4: Assess existing mechanisms for transboundary cooperation to improve resilient water management and reduce community vulnerability to climate change, including existing regional organizations (such as ACTO) and other bilateral/multilateral agreements.

### Technical assistance components (Total cost: USD 65 million; GCF cost: USD 18 million)

This component will create activities to make mechanisms for the inclusion of gender and diversity, including women, Indigenous populations, adolescents, persons with disabilities, and other vulnerable groups such as older persons in order to ensure inclusivity of the interventions across the participating countries.

### Monitoring and evaluation components (Total cost: USD 6.75 million; GCF cost: USD 4.00 million)

This will cover the relevant activities required to monitor and evaluate the programme’s impact, including a baseline assessment at the start of the project and regular monitoring during its implementation, with respect to both the GCF outcome and project level.

### Project management (Total cost: USD 5.50 million; GCF cost: USD 1.00 million)

The total project management cost, including the co-financing portion, is 9.4% of the total project budget, which is in accordance with the general principles and indicative list of eligible costs covered under the GCF Policy on fees for accredited entities and delivery partners.

## Assessment of performance against investment criteria

### Impact potential: Medium to high


---
**Page 118**
---

3.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: Medium to high

The programme seeks to integrate climate risk management into regional policies and infrastructure development in a way that is both cost-effective and sustainable. This will demonstrate scalable, climate-resilient infrastructure models that can be replicated across the Amazon Basin and beyond. By establishing a finance facility and showcasing successful adaptation strategies, it aims to facilitate sector-level investment and policy adoption, facilitating broader regional and global replication.

3.3 Sustainable development potential Scale: High

The programme supports multiple Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) through its comprehensive approach to climate resilience in the Amazon Basin. SDG 1 (No poverty) and SDG 10 (Reduced inequalities) are advanced by promoting sustainable livelihoods and reducing vulnerability to climate-related economic shocks, particularly for marginalized communities. SDG 5 (Gender equality) is supported by initiatives that promote women's participation in climate-adaptation efforts and decision-making processes. SDG 6 (Clean water and sanitation) is directly tackled by improving water security and sanitation infrastructure.

SDG 11 (Sustainable cities and communities) is advanced through the programme’s focus on enhancing urban resilience to climate change, with a particular emphasis as one of the initiative's strengths in its emphasis on climate adaptation and resilience-building. SDG 15 (Life on land) is supported by the programme's EBA approaches and nature-based solutions, which help protect biodiversity while contributing to broader development goals.

By promoting sustainable development that recognizes complex relationships between climate, environment, and human well-being in the Amazon Basin.

3.4 Needs of recipients Scale: High

The need for GCF funding for this programme is clearly demonstrated through its role in enhancing climate resilience within existing activities and investments. The funding will specifically address a critical gap in current infrastructure and service delivery. This financial support is essential for transforming conventional WSS approaches into climate-resilient systems that can withstand increasing environmental pressures in the region.

3.5 Country ownership Scale: High

The programme demonstrates strong country ownership through its close alignment with the national development priorities of the target countries in the Amazon Basin. This alignment ensures that the programme's objectives and activities are in harmony with each country’s development agenda, thereby maximizing impact. Additionally, the regional coordination facilitated by ACTO reinforces the collaborative nature of the programme, fostering a sense of shared ownership among participating countries. This regional approach


---
**Page 119**
---

not only enhances the effectiveness of climate adaptation efforts but also strengthens existing cooperation frameworks in the Amazon region.

25. The development of the programme proposal was initiated in response to a gap identified by the Amazon countries themselves, underscoring its relevance to local needs and priorities. Throughout the process, extensive consultations were held with stakeholders at bilateral meetings were conducted throughout the proposal development process. These consultations provided valuable feedback on the design of the programme, including the structure of the programme's components, and ensure that it addresses the specific climate challenges faced by their countries. The strong engagement in this process has resulted in a high level of ownership among the participating countries, increasing the likelihood of successful implementation and long-term sustainability of the climate-resilience efforts in the Amazon Basin.

### 3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness Scale: Medium

25. The proposal offers a high-quality and rigorous economic and financial analysis (EFA), effectively demonstrating the programme's efficiency and effectiveness. The EFA identifies interventions, each requiring direct capital and operational expenditures across the six targeted countries. These include water supply and sanitation, solid waste management, urban water, rural water, and green drainage.

25. Long-term economic viability is supported by positive economic indicators across most interventions. For example, water supply and sanitation projects demonstrate significant health outcomes, including reductions in infant mortality, healthcare cost savings, time savings in water collection, and increased school attendance. These projects also show positive economic net present values and economic internal rates of return (IRR) exceeding country-specific discount rates. Urban water management systems are the most economically viable, requiring full GCF concessional support through grants, while rural water management is the most visible, with economic IRRs above 25 per cent across the board.

26. Financial viability over the 25-year period, based on the financial modeling conducted for this proposal, is generally positive in all six countries, except for urban water management systems; although results vary. Urban interventions demonstrate profitability, whereas rural ones are either marginally profitable or unprofitable depending on the discount rate and country-specific discount rates. Each subproject's financial structure includes a mix of GCF grants, GCF loans, and IGF loans. The fund allocation methodology is well-defined, setting minimum financial performance targets for each intervention to ensure that maximum economic efficiency and optimization was achieved by targeting the minimum necessary level of GCF intervention.

26. Concessionality was comprehensively assessed during the appraisal process. For GCF, a low concessional rate of 10 per cent is proposed for all six countries, as highlighted in the EFA, and the target countries' macroeconomic conditions. IGF loans following the same criteria are also proposed at a low concessional rate. The proposal includes a margin lending and spreading. The AE confirms that the two loans will not be blended, and GCF concessional support is provided only for grants. The AE also states that the average service coverage ratios for most subprojects are expected to be around 12%, ensuring adequate cash reflux in all six countries.

26. The adaptation cost per direct beneficiary is USD 150, and USD 286 per total beneficiary, positioning it within the median range of the GCF adaptation portfolio for Latin America and the Caribbean (LAC). The programme proposal also aims to establish a portfolio of "best practices in the Amazon Basin", with financial performance to be closely monitored throughout programme implementation.


---
**Page 120**
---

### IV. Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### 4.1 Environmental and social safeguards

**Programme overview:** The programme aims to: (i) increase climate resilience in water resources management through enhanced preparedness and response to extreme climate events; (ii) improve resilience in ecosystem services and carbon stocks of sociocultural systems and livelihoods, including the Amazon Basin; and (iii) contribute to increased economic resilience and low-carbon development in the Amazon Basin. The programme's social co-benefits include improved access to water resources for rural communities, a decrease in the mortality rate due to infections and parasitic diseases in children, (ii) the provision of safe water and sanitation services, which can improve child health outcomes, and therefore, health. Economic co-benefits are also related to improving children's health because of the increased access to clean water and sanitation.

**Environmental and social risk category as safeguard instrument:** The programme will provide a comprehensive assessment of environmental and social risks in the Amazon Basin for the building of capacity and systems, and the development of a policy framework and investment plans on water resources. The financing of projects under the programme will be in the form of grants or loans to national governments, international organizations, and financial intermediaries for the funding of a portfolio of projects. The AE has assigned an environmental and social risk category (ESRC) to each project consistent with the risk category and it is within the AE's environmental and social risk accreditation level. The AE has prepared an Environmental and Social Management Framework (ESMF) for the programme.

**Compliance with GCF’s Operational and Social Safeguards (OSS) standards:**

- **Paragraph 30:** This paragraph describes how the programme complies with the standards.
  
- **ESS1 Assessment and Management of Environmental and Social Risks and Impacts:** The AE has submitted an Environmental and Social Analysis and an ESMF and a Strategy for Mitigation of Environmental and Social Risks and Impacts. The analysis covers the social and environmental profile of the Amazon Basin, the prevailing issues and contextual risks, and the potential environmental and social impacts associated with the programme activities.

- **ESS2 Labor and Working Conditions:** The standard on Labor and Working Conditions is applicable to all projects under the programme that are implemented in or through facilities. The ESMF has identified some general and indicative examples of potential risks associated with child labour, forced labour, exposure to hazardous substances and materials, and inadequate health and safety conditions at work. In relation to working conditions, the ESMS mentions that subjects will adopt and implement labour management policies and procedures which are aligned with the standard, as well as an occupational health and safety policy. The ESMF also identifies some specific risks associated with the classes of hazards, including physical, chemical, biological, and radiological hazards and risks of accidents or injuries at work. Subjects will adopt and implement appropriate practices and working conditions and guidelines for evaluation of compliance with the standard.


---
**Page 121**
---

38. **ESSS: Resource Efficiency and Pollution Prevention** The programme will increase water availability through proper aquifer management. Water use is expected to be minimal as the programme will promote nature-based ESSA type subprojects. In terms of pollution, the subprojects will reduce contamination of soil, surface water and groundwater from sewage and organic matter. The design of subprojects should take into account the proximity of receptors (settlements, water sources, and critical habitats), and the risk of natural hazards, including tectonic hazards, wildfires, landslides and flood events. If not properly designed, these risks could lead to increased exposure of communities to environmental hazards.

Air pollution may also occur during the operation of sewage treatment and solid waste facilities in the future. The ESF will ensure that subprojects comply with national air quality standards. The risk of temporary air and water quality deterioration due to the emission of dust and particulates from construction activities can be mitigated by following best practices. To address these risks, the ESF mentions that subprojects will comply with country regulations and permit requirements as well as with World Health Organization (WHO) guidelines. The ESF also mentions various management plans (e.g., Waste Management and Demolition, Quarry Exploration and Waste Disposal, Garbage Accumulation Mitigation in Canada and Environmentally Sustainable Suburban Development). In addition to these measures, where necessary, specific management measures for the subprojects.

39. **ESSS: Environmental Security** Communities will potentially be exposed to hazards during the construction of infrastructure or facilities, including the risk of traffic accidents, exposure to hazardous materials, waterborne diseases, vector-borne diseases, and other environmental risks. The ESF will ensure that subprojects comply with national regulations including security personnel. There are also risks to local communities from natural hazards, including landslides, floods, wildfires, earthquakes, tsunamis, and volcanic eruptions due to poor location or inadequate design of infrastructure. The ESF indicates that measures to manage these risks include the development of sectoral ESSS checklists and guidelines. These will be used as Vector-borne Diseases Programme, Traffic Control Programme, Disaster and Climate Change Management Programme, Environmental Security Subprogramme, and other relevant programmes. They also support the development of sectoral ESSS checklists and guidelines. Subprojects will be required to develop and implement measures to prevent and mitigate risks of gender-based violence, including sexual exploitation and abuse (SEA) against women and girls, as well as violence against persons in the community or other subproject workers, by adopting a code of conduct for working with vulnerable groups.

40. **ESSS: Land Acquisition and Involuntary Resettlement** The construction of infrastructure and facilities may require the acquisition of land or restrictions on land use and access to resources (e.g., water sources). This can lead to involuntary resettlement or displacement (i.e. relocation of homes), and/or economic displacement (i.e. loss of land, assets, or existing livelihoods) for communities that are not directly affected by the project (e.g., water sources or other means of livelihood). However, the programme will avoid or minimize involuntary resettlement through the development of alternative subproject designs. Proposers of subprojects that have involuntary resettlement impacts will be required to develop and implement a Resettlement Action Plan (RAP) or an Alternative RAP (ARP). There will be full engagement with subproject affected people during the planning and implementation of the RAP/ARP Subprogramme that cause large-scale physical and/or economic displacement. The ESF will ensure that all involuntary resettlement impacts are scheduled from being financed under the programme.

41. **ESSS: Conservation and Sustainable Management of Living Natural Resources** The ESF has identified the following potential impacts of subprojects to be supported under the programme: (a) loss of vegetation cover; (b) disturbance of fauna; (c) conversion of natural habitats; (d) degradation of water sources; (e) destruction of internationally recognized natural habitats and critical habitats, and internationally recognized areas such as key

### Stakeholder engagement and information disclosure

At the programme level, the proposed approach to stakeholder engagement is based on a multi-stakeholder platform that includes technical staff from ACTO member countries, the Global Water Partnership (GWP), national designated authorities, and other relevant international agencies. Systematic engagements were also undertaken by the AE with different actors including Indigenous groups, Afrodescendants, women's associations, chambers, start-up enterprises, foundations, and local and international NGOs. The engagement process is designed to ensure that all stakeholders are kept informed of progress in the preparation which updates programme-level stakeholder engagement objectives, criteria for selection of key stakeholders, and a list of potential stakeholders. Each project will be required to develop and implement an SEP aimed at promoting and providing the means for effective and inclusive engagement with stakeholders, especially subproject-affected people.

### Cultural heritage

The cultural heritage component is not included as part of the subject environmental and social assessment. However, removal or significant damage to critical cultural heritage will not be allowed under the programme. When the proposed project involves a site that has been identified by UNESCO as World Heritage, the subprojects will identify and assess potential risks during subproject construction and operation on the basis of information provided in the relevant documentation.

### Implementation arrangements

The AE will assign an environmental, social and governance (ESG) specialist to each specific Category B subject that is prepared under the programme. The ESG specialist may be a member of the AE team or hired as needed. External consultants may also be hired to provide support when needed. Each subproject will have its own ESG specialist who will work with the AE and project staff as needed. This ESG specialist will prepare reports, establish the level of detail and periodicity of supervision and monitoring through periodic reports, site visits, and specific environmental assessments, which are mentioned in the proposal and implementation arrangements.

### Indigenous Peoples

In ES7: Indigenous Peoples, there is presence of Indigenous Peoples (IPs) in all the countries involved in the programme. The AE has provided an IP Framework Plan to comply with the UNDRIP and other international instruments that IPs have ratified or endorsed that so require. The Exclusion List ensures that lands, natural resources, cultural and natural heritage sites, and sacred places are not impacted by the project. It is recognized that Indigenous Peoples are not relocated away from their lands and natural resources, and that Indigenous Peoples in isolation or initial contact are not impacted by the project. The AE is recommended to consult with the relevant IPs and other stakeholders through the Indigenous Peoples Advisory Group on relevant Indigenous Peoples' issues that may be encountered.
