### Gender policy

The GCF provided a gender assessment and action plan with the funding proposal, therefore complies with the requirements of the GCF Updated Gender Policy. The gender assessment highlights significant gendered barriers faced by women in the Amazonas region, which stem from their limited access to resources and decision-making power.

Women are primarily responsible for household water collection, which restricts their ability to participate fully in community activities. Women also face physical and social barriers caused by insufficient access to clean water and sanitation facilities, making women and girls more vulnerable to environmental risks such as flooding and landslides. Additionally, women have limited access to land tenure, are underrepresented in decision-making processes, and face gender-based violence, which further restricts their participation in community activities, leading to increased vulnerability to environmental risks. These challenges are deeply rooted in gender norms that marginalize women and limit their ability to participate fully in decision-making power.


---
**Page 124**
---

### 4.3 Risks

#### 4.3.1 Accredited entity/executing entity capability to execute the current programme (medium risk)

Subproject/subject executing entities (EEs) will be determined ex post during programme implementation based on the indicative list of already identified EEs included in the funding proposal, some EEIs may not be able to comply with IBF standards in terms of capacity or availability of resources. The Secretariat will monitor the progress and quality of implementing implementation and the programme's reach and impact could be lower than expected. IDB will conduct a mid-term review to assess the approval of each subproject and provide the evidence to GCF as described in the terms of reference.

#### 4.3.2 Programme-specific execution risks (low risk)

##### Political instability: Complex political and social dynamics could alter government priorities, regulations or the level of support for the programme, and cause delays in implementation. The Secretariat will work with representatives of each country to manage potential changes as has described in the funding proposal’s management approach in countries where political risk is identified as significant.

##### Credit risk: GCF’s loan of USD 10 million will be provided to the sovereign government of each country for project execution and repayment of the sovereign credit. Credit risk differs among the six participating countries as shown by their sovereign rating by Moody’s Investors Service (Moodys). The ratings are as follows: Brazil (BBB+/Ba2), Colombia (BBB-/Ba3), Ecuador (BB+/Ba1) and Suriname (CCC+/P-1). In October 2024, the outlook for Brazil was upgraded to Ba1. In October 2024, the outlook for Peru was stable at BB+ and the outlook for Suriname was upgraded to BBB-.


---
**Page 125**
---

### 4.4 Fiduciary

1. The programme will be supervised by IIB as an AE. At the subproject level, and implementation will be governed by IIB procedures and policies.
2. Components 1, 2 and 3 will be implemented through a set of subprojects approved by IIB in accordance with its internal policies and procedures, and aligned with programme's eligibility criteria.
3. Activities will be implemented either by third-party EEs or IIBs, according to its nature and complexity. The IIB will ensure that the activities are carried out in compliance with the given activities and according to the criteria presented in the funded activity agreement. IIB will assess their technical, financial and institutional capacity to fulfil the role prior to entering into any arrangement.
4. IIB will carry out all appropriate due diligence on each subproject, including all checks and assessments required by IIB's internal policies and procedures as well as those of executing entities. It is the responsibility of IIB to ensure that EEs monitor and collect the data required for the proper execution of the monitoring and evaluation plan, in accordance with the corresponding programme guidelines and the relevant requirements at the subproject level.

**Page 126**

## Results monitoring and reporting

As an adaptation initiative, the programme aims at generating diverse adaptation results across all four GCF adaptation result areas. It is estimated that 1,522,249 direct beneficiaries and a 4,108,376 indirect beneficiaries will be reached by the project activities, which together account for 18.5 per cent of 54,133,377 inhabitants of the Amazon Basin.

Definitions and methodological bases have been provided by the AE to estimate the adaptation results. At this stage, it was not possible to provide a detailed breakdown without detailed information about subprojects. It is laudable that the AE has proposed the methodology dividing the project into four result areas: climate change, environment, etc.

The theory of change diagram adequately captures different levels of expected changes as well as logical linkages between them from the goal statement to the proposed activities. It is good that the AE has provided a detailed explanation on how the project will contribute towards climate results and captures the causal relationships between outcomes and co-benefits. However, it was not possible to provide an assessment at this stage because it was not clearly depicted in the theory of change diagram, but the AE has clarified and agreed to measure the change as a part of an enabling environment, particularly using indicator 7.

The project is expected to achieve the intended results at all levels: national, subnational, and local. It is of good quality with adequate GCF Integrated Results Management Framework indicators and as well as a set of programme-specific indicators which can properly monitor the intended results at both outcome and output levels.

## Legal assessment

The Accreditation Master Agreement was signed with the Accredited Entity on 29 August 2017, and became effective on 30 March 2018, which was amended and rotated pursuant to a first amendment and restatement agreement dated 14 April 2024, and which became effective on 15 May 2024.

The Accredited Entity has not provided a legal opinion/certificate confirming that it has obtained all internal approvals, and it has the capacity and authority to implement the programme.

---

## The Fund Proposal Describes Several Implementation Options for the Programme

Under such circumstances, the Borrower/Grantee shall not be required to enter directly into agreements with the Accredited Entity (the "Borrower/Grantee") may not be the entity that is implementing the programme. In this case, the Borrower/Grantee will need to enter into separate agreements with each of the Implementing Entities. The Borrower/Grantee shall make all reasonable efforts to enter into agreements directly with each Implementing Entity. If it may not always be feasible for the Accredited Entity to do so, in such a scenario, there will only be an agreement between the Borrower/Grantee and the Accredited Entity (the "Execution Agreement"), with the Borrower/Grantee entering into a separate execution agreement with the Implementing Entity (the "Implementation Agreement"). The Implementation Agreement shall not include any reference to the Execution Agreement. The Accredited Entity will not be a party to the Execution Agreement. However, the terms of the Execution Agreement may refer to the Implementation Agreement.

### Absence of Direct Legal Relationship

The absence of a direct legal relationship between the Accredited Entity and the Implementing Entity means that the Accredited Entity does not have direct recourse against the Implementing Entity for any breach or non-performance by such entity. If the Borrower/Grantee has agreed in the term sheet that the Borrower/Grantee will be accountable for all the actions of the Implementing Entity, then the Borrower/Grantee shall ensure that it is clear to the Borrower/Grantee that the obligations of the Implementing Entity shall be fulfilled to the satisfaction of the Accredited Entity.

### Request for Provisions in AMA

The Accredited Entity has requested that the provisions of the AMA allow GCF to step into the Accredited Entity's contractual arrangements with Borrowers/Grantees by disclaimer. The Board is advised that such disclaimer would not include any liabilities not included in the Accredited Entity's financing agreements and the Accredited Entity is not able to provide a definitive statement on whether such disclaimer would be acceptable to sovereign entities and the nature of such contractual arrangements (agreements with sovereign entities). The Secretariat has agreed to the Accredited Entity's request.

### Conditions for Board Approval

The Board is recommended that any approval by the Board is made subject to the following conditions:

- Submission by the Accredited Entity to the Fund of a certificate or legal opinion, in form and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat within 120 days after the Board confirms that the Accredited Entity has obtained all final internal approvals needed by it and any other relevant authorities.
- Signature of the funded activity agreement in a form and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat within 180 days from the date of Board approval, or the date the Accredited Entity has obtained all final internal approvals, whichever is later; and
- Completion of the legal diligence to the satisfaction of the GCF Secretariat.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP261

## Proposal name:
Improving Climate Resilience by Increasing Water Security in the Amazon Basin

## Accredited entity:
Inter-American Development Bank

## Country(ies):
Bolivia (Plurinational State of), Brazil, Colombia, Ecuador, Peru, Suriname

## Programme size:
Large

### I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

1. The Amazon basin faces significant impacts from climate change, including floods, droughts and fires, which will be increasingly exacerbated by extreme rainfall events and high temperatures. This assessment is based on the following assumptions: improved water resources management policies, enhance early warning systems and develop more robust water, sanitation and waste (WSW) infrastructure.

2. The programme, to be implemented in its Amazon Cooperation Treaty Organization (ACTO) signature States (Bolivia [Plurinational State of], Brazil, Colombia, Ecuador, Peru and Suriname), aims at improving climate resilience by strengthening water management across the Amazon basin.

3. The three programme components seek to:
   - Increase climate resilience in water resources management through enhanced preparedness and response to extreme climatic events in the Amazon basin (outcome 1).
   - Improve the resilience, ecosystem services and carbon stocks of Amazonian socio-ecological systems by strengthening their adaptive capacity (outcome 2).
   - Strengthen governance and the enabling environment in order to foster climate resilience and low-carbon development in the Amazon basin (outcome 3).

4. The programme components are defined as a combination of several subprojects, each representing a single activity or a set of activities under the respective component. The subprojects are to be approved by the accredited entity (AE), the Inter-American Development Bank (IDB), in accordance with the IDB's criteria for project approval and its eligibility guidelines.

5. The execution modalities differ between the programme components:
   - Executing entities under component 1 will be ministries, water agencies and hydrological-climate monitoring institutions involved in water resources management and hydrological-climate monitoring.
   - Executing entities under component 2 will be ministries of housing, water and sanitation, development and planning, as well as national development banks or other infrastructure financing institutions responsible for the provision and/or prevention of water, sanitation and solid waste management services; and
   - The executing entity under component 3 is envisaged to be ACTO.

## In its assessment, the independent Technical Advisory Panel (ITAP) considered the document package as submitted on 15 November 2024, along with written responses by 6 and 13 December 2024. The ITAP meeting was held in a virtual format and followed up during a Zoom call between the ITAP, the AE, and the GCF Secretariat on 10 December 2024.

### Impact potential

**Scale: Medium to High**

The proposed programme aims to enhance the resilience of vulnerable communities, including Indigenous Peoples, and key ecosystems in the Amazon basin to the anticipated impacts of climate change. The expected results are that the resilience of these communities will be achieved through an adaptation-focused and integrated water management approach, and the promotion or restoration of the ecosystem’s capacity to provide essential hydrological services (fluvial and groundwater).

The advances will be measured in four GCF Results Areas for adaptation: AR41 Most vulnerable people and Communities, including Indigenous Peoples; AR42 Health, well-being.


---
**Page 130**
---

food, water security, ARA1 Infrastructure and built environment, and ARA4 Ecosystem and ecosystem services” (funding proposal, para. 9).

As explained in the funding proposal and its annexes [22], the proposed programme’s interventions are estimated to benefit more than 1.5 million people directly. With a basin population of approximately 54 million people, this means that direct beneficiaries is almost 3 per cent of the population of the Amazon basin. Indirect benefits are estimated at around 600,000 people (para. 9). The number of indirect and/or multiple beneficiaries is based on the nationwide female ratio of the population of the participating countries.

Approximately 1.36 million people are estimated to benefit directly from the early warning systems that are to be strengthened under component 1. This estimate is based on an assumption that each household in a high-risk sub-basin will have at least one member who benefits directly. The number of indirect beneficiaries of this component, 3.6 million people, is calculated by assuming that every household in the basin has access to early warning systems.

The number of direct beneficiaries of component 2 activities, nearly 180,000 people, corresponds to people in communities with a high risk of drought, who are estimated to benefit from interventions aimed at improving water management and increasing agricultural productivity and land restoration, subject to budget availability. The number of indirect beneficiaries of this component is calculated as the estimated population at a high risk of flooding in the corresponding sub-basin.

The potential number of beneficiaries from component 3 activities has not been assessed yet. However, it should be noted that the programme will create enabling policies and catalyse integrated approaches and transboundary cooperation; there may arguably be an additional benefit to the basin as a whole in terms of its capacity to foster climate resilience and low-carbon development in the Amazon basin (outcome 3).

The ITAP positively notes that the programme is concerned with the production of “specific” results, which are defined in section 1.2 below. However, this has not been consistently applied in the estimation of potential beneficiaries.

The ITAP also recognises the inherent difficulties in assessing and quantifying the benefits from components 4 to 6 (see para. 9). Noting that the actual interventions of the programme will depend upon which specific subjects become reality, and with the caveat that this is a work-in-progress, the ITAP notes that the programme implementation could potentially reach greater numbers of both direct and indirect beneficiaries.

Further, the ITAP sees opportunities for increased learning, information exchange and communication through working with qualitative indicators and information for programme activity monitoring and evaluation. This will be further elaborated upon in section 2 below and is reiterated in recommendation 1 in section III below.

One of the anticipated co-benefits is an increase in carbon sinks from land restoration and natural regeneration activities. The estimated impact contribution to this is 7.77 million t CO₂, over the 20-year lifetime of the programme.

The CO₂ sequestration will be achieved through the NBS implemented through the subprojects under components 4-6 (see para. 9). The actual quantity of CO₂ sequestered and stored will be largely dependent upon how well these subprojects are implemented, as well as on the overall success of the programme’s overall focus on adaption and that the increase in carbon sinks is a co-benefit.

Given that the subprojects are yet to be defined, it is not possible to gauge with any certainty what the actual contribution will be. This will be further elaborated upon in section 1 below, where the eligibility criteria for subproject selection are still under elaboration

# Paradigm shift potential

## Scale: Medium to High

The programme recognizes the complexity and systemic nature of climate risks affecting the Amazon basin. For this reason, a comprehensive intervention approach with actions across the entire system has been designed. The three components are highly complementary:

- **Component 1** takes steps towards the development of the Impact-Based Multi-Hazard Early Warning System (IB-MH-EWS) to better anticipate and mitigate impacts of climate hazards;
- **Component 2** implements subprojects for WSW water technologies (and infrastructures), as well as small, medium and large communities (urban centers). This will involve water conservation, improving water efficiency, water reuse and protection from contamination, and the implementation of Ecosystem-Based Adaptation (EbA) strategies, including wetland restoration, riparian buffer creation, reforestation, ordination of protected areas, and the sustainable generation of aquifer recharge zones and hence climate-resilient development; and
- **Component 3** supports the development of national and regional policy frameworks by promoting a regulatory environment conducive to enabling environmental and transboundary cooperation. This contributes to the enabling environment to incentivize climate-proofing investments in river basins towards the application of NDCs and climate-resilient approaches.

The programme's contribution to an enabling environment is or strengthening the regulatory framework and policies will, however, be limited by the small amount of funds dedicated to component 3. With only 2.3 per cent of the total budget, including both grants and co-financing, this component is not expected to have a significant impact.

The funding proposal suggests "The enhancement will be achievable and traceable through the following measurable changes:

- *Number of new or revised policies and frameworks that are enacted and promote climate-resilient development*
- *Formal transboundary cooperation legal agreements and mechanisms established between basin countries, with protocols for data exchange, monitoring systems and resource management.*
- *Development of watershed transboundary master planning along with coordinated conservation and restoration actions in the Amazon Basin.*
- *Creation of financial mechanisms that support long-term investments in climate adaptation, leveraging local and international green finance for ecosystem-based approaches.*
- *Capacity-building efforts resulting in increased numbers of trained stakeholders (disaggregated by gender) who can access and manage climate finance* (funding proposal, para. 93).

24. As mentioned in paragraph 16 above, the ITAP sees opportunities for increased learning, information exchange and communication through working with qualitative indicators and information for programme activity implementation and monitoring. The achievements listed in paragraph 17 above are a good starting point to understand how these indicators can have positive effects on the enabling policy environment in the Amazon basin. A systematic use of qualitative data, as proposed by recommendation 3 (see section II below), will help to better understand what policy mechanisms work in the Amazon basin context and can be used for learning and communication.

25. The potential for knowledge creation is considerable:
(a) Component 1 develops the understanding of climate impacts on the hydrological system and its impacts on local communities (e.g., flooding, drought, water quality). It also seeks to understand how these impacts are being managed.
(b) Component 2 advances technical knowledge and understanding of new technologies and the use of EIA and NIS, along with insight into ways to sustainably fund climate interventions.
(c) Component 3 builds awareness and knowledge at the political, financial and community levels about the regulatory environment in order to further guide the focus of investments.

26. The community-based participatory monitoring envisaged as activity 1.2-4 will allow the recognition of changes in the hydrological system and its impacts on local communities through qualitative and quantitative environmental change (see recommendation 1 in section II below). The activity seeks to ensure that "local perspectives contribute to decision-making processes and related policy development" (see recommendation 3, paragraph 50 of the ITAP proposal, para. 44).

27. The programme's potential to catalyse change beyond a one-off investment can be assessed in relation to the plans and measures for sustainability, replication and scaling up. The programme emphasises developing the capacity of the executing entities, since the relevant ministries and agencies are not yet equipped with the skills or knowledge required. This capacity is to be assured by developing a finance facility to channel climate investments into water and sanitation.

28. The anticipated finance facility for the Amazon region is to channel investments into climate-resilient WSW management. Concretely, the facility will support EIA interventions under conditions that ensure their sustainability beyond the programme period (e.g., through training to generate EIA solutions beyond the programme). In the question and answer exchange between the ITAP and AE, it was noted that some of these activities (e.g., capacity building for conservation activities) already operate in the region, whereas other innovative mechanisms have yet to be developed.

29. Given that the majority of the programme is dedicated to the financing of WSW subprojects that integrate EIA and NIS, the programme should generate valuable experience on how to deliver these interventions within the context of climate-resilient water and sanitation activities. The ITAP proposes that the AE emphasises the production and dissemination of learning from this experience in order to support other programmes in the region (see recommendation 3 in section II below).

30. It was noted in the question and answer exchange between the ITAP and the AE that delays in the delivery of equipment may be a challenge for users that may also encounter challenges with regard to the equipment's maintenance. In order to ensure the successful implementation of the envisaged EIA interventions, it is recommended that the programme should encourage implementing entities to hand over all installations and equipment to the relevant agency or organization responsible for its use and maintenance as early as possible. # Sustainable development

## Scale: Medium to High

### Activity 1.2.3 establishes gender & socially inclusive advisory services (funding proposals for these services are included in the annexes). This is a necessary step but may be insufficient for an effective end-to-end and people-centred early warning system.

The IB-MHEWS should also issue warnings on threats to people and the environment related to long-term ecological and land-use changes. Authorities and communities need to know, and practice, how to respond in order for the IB-MHEWS to be fully effective.

### Activity 13.2 establishes a national greenhouse gas emissions inventory and stores through the NBS and EAs activities of component 2. These activities include forest preservation and restoration. Moreover, to the extent that pollution monitoring and regulation will be improved as part of the IB-MHEWS, this will also contribute to reducing pollution loads in the Amazon River Basin.

### Activity 13.4 provides access to clean water and sanitation, SDG 11 (sustainable cities and communities) and SDG 6 (clean water and sanitation). This activity contributes directly to achieving SDGs 5 (gender equality) and SDG 15 (life on land).

### Activity 13.5 provides access to improved sanitation and hygiene, which will reduce the transmission of infectious diseases.

The programme seeks to improve the basin-level hydrological and meteorological network and make IB-MHEWS available to facilitate forecast-based action and respond in time to extreme events. The system should be able to collect, process, store, and analyse both rapid and slower changes over time and will give appropriate warnings on droughts, fires and critical water levels. In addition to the use of quantitative data from meteorological stations and qualitative community-based ecological information and data, the system should also issue warnings on threats to people and the environment related to long-term ecological and land-use changes.

Altogether, the program shift potential is assessed as medium to high.


---
**Page 134**
---

### Economic co-benefits are not directly highlighted in the funding proposal, but the investment and improvement of WASH systems using NHW and an EHA approach will strengthen livelihoods and income-earning opportunities. These co-benefits will depend upon which, and how, solutions are prioritized. In particular, investing in water infrastructure to support vulnerable communities may be the most efficient way to enhance the population's resilience to climate change.

### In relation to women’s empowerment and the potential for gender-sensitive or transformative funding, the funding proposal pays close attention to involving and training women, particularly those who have been historically marginalized (e.g., women with disabilities). However, in areas where women are overlooked (e.g. in training on hygiene and sanitation practices), there is a risk of inadvertent exclusion. In the written questions and answer exchange between the ITAP and the AE, and the ITAP is convinced that the AI is committed to training sanitation and hygiene as a shared responsibility across genders.

### The funding proposal also highlights co-benefits from improved water access, increased income-earning opportunities from improved access to water and the commensurate reduction in the collection time for water. This includes the potential for women to participate more fully in economic activities that are currently limited by their time spent collecting water. The programme aims to empower women, who are disproportionately affected by water-related challenges. Additionally, improved WASH systems would enable women and girls to manage menstrual hygiene more effectively.

### In terms of education, the funding proposal highlights co-benefits in terms of reduced school dropout rates among adolescent girls.

### Alongside this, the sustainable development potential is assessed as medium to high.

#### 1.4 Needs of the recipient

| Scale: Medium to High |
| --- |

* The Amazon basin faces significant impacts, including droughts, floods and heatwaves, all of which are exacerbated by climate change.
* Water-intensive or water-dependent activities, such as agriculture, livestock production, and fishing, are particularly vulnerable in this region. Indigenous Peoples and local communities (IPLCs) living in the Amazon region, who are highly sensitive to temperature and precipitation fluctuations, are also at risk from these impacts.

### The programme’s countries [five upper-middle-income countries and one lower-middle-income country] should be able to provide funding for many of the collective pursuits of the programme. This is consistent with the appropriate that 70 per cent of the consolidated programme budget is in the form of loans.

### The Amazon region has a high level of climate vulnerability compared to other regions at similar income levels, and it is in many ways more exposed to climate hazards than the national averages. The high level of climate vulnerability and problematic social and economic conditions are reflected in the relatively low Human Development Index (HDI) scores for the region.

### Within the Amazon region, the programme prioritizes WASH interventions in vulnerable communities, particularly those lacking adequate infrastructure, to ensure equitable access to climate-resilient systems. In the written questions and answer exchange between the ITAP and the AE, the ITAP is convinced that the AI is committed to involving people with disabilities and ethno-engineering (culture adaptation) for Indigenous Peoples and people of African descent.

### In relation to the assessment of environmental and social risks impacts, the funding proposal [table 4, row ESFIC, p. 05] confirms “Throughout the lifespan of the cycle, an impact assessment will be conducted in accordance with the guidelines established and maintained based on meaningful consultation in a culturally appropriate manner.”

**1.5 Country ownership**

* **Scale:** Medium to High

The programme responds to a unified request by the eight signatory countries of ACTO, directed at the AE.

Ownership at the country level is crucial in order to ensure appropriate aims and long-term results. Reportedly, since the creation of the programme's concept note, the appropriation of the programme has been promoted with all beneficiary countries, which will continue during the implementation period.

National priorities, strategies and plans were submitted by the Plurinational State of Bolivia, Brazil, Colombia, Ecuador, Peru and Suriname. Guyana was included in the preparations for the funding request. The Republic of Paraguay (Paraguayan National Institute of Water Resources [Bolivarian Republic]) is not eligible for IDH funding; hence, the programme is to be implemented only by the other six countries.

The AE, IDH, works widely in the region and possesses in-depth experience and a track record of financing and implementing projects and programmes. The experience and track record of the AE are considered as an added value for the implementation of this defined. Notwithstanding, if capacity to implement is lacking, there is ample opportunity for support and training from the AE.

Stakeholder consultation has been conducted at various stages of the programme's development and is comprehensively reported on in annex 7 (summary of consultations and stakeholder engagement).

An important finding from these consultations read: "The active involvement of local and Indigenous communities in water resource management is indispensable for sustainability in the Amazon region. The AE will support the countries to strengthen their capacity in river basin management and protection of these resources. Establishing networks of local organizations can strengthen the role of civil society, which has a key role in the implementation of funding programs, and advocacy campaigns play a critical role in shaping policies and securing necessary institutional reforms."

The programme also has a highly developed environmental and social policy framework, comprising, as appropriate, an environmental and social management system, environmental and social impact assessment procedures, and a monitoring and evaluation plan. The consideration of environmental and social risks focuses on affected communities and the environment.

Without compromising the AE's responsibility to fully consider environmental and social risks and avoid harm, the ITAP also sees opportunities for realizing, for example, the "full respect for the human rights, collective rights, dignity, aspirations, culture, and natural

# Efficiency and effectiveness

## Scale: Medium

An economic and financial analysis was conducted (and is reported on in annexes 3 and 7 to the funding proposal). Examples of 10 subprojects are included in the funding proposal, of which it achieves a debt service coverage ratio of above 1.2, which should ensure the repayment of debt.

As emphasized throughout this assessment, the configuration of the subprojects to be implemented under this Programme is such that they will not introduce new technologies or alter the system or only WSP technologies and infrastructure without sufficient consideration of regulatory requirements, environmental impacts, and social considerations. The criteria on the process and criteria for how the subprojects of the programme will be rolled out are:

### Recommendations 2 and 5 in section II below suggest that the accredited entity complement the eligibility criteria with additional criteria to ensure that the projects are aligned with the national policy.

The eligibility criteria for the selection of the subprojects are to be fully developed in the funding proposal (annexes 3, 7, and 8) and will be reported on in annexes 2.1 and 2.3).

Beyond considerations of an appropriate balance between the countries, the subprojects are to be selected among proposals that meet general and specific criteria, such as:

- Technical feasibility
- Alignment with national policy, including the NDC, LTS, NAP, and national laws and frameworks that address climate change
- Local policies and institutional project environments. Projects under this Programme must align with local policies to ensure that the necessary regulatory and business models are in place for the subprojects to be implemented and share and manage the risk of new technologies for WSP and EWSR investments.
- Contribution to improved transboundary water cooperation and management. Since the Programme is designed as a pilot project, it is not possible at this time to require that the Basin, transboundary cooperation and management are desirable; therefore, transboundary projects will be given preference
- Comply with the Environmental and Social Criteria set out in the ESMP...
- Project-specific metrics... related particularly to Component 2, outputs 2.1 and 2.2* (funding proposal, annexes 3, pp. 213–214).


---
**Page 137**
---

Project-specific criteria, strategic objectives of the programme and project categories

| Project specific criteria | Strategic objectives of the Programme |
| --- | --- |
| Projects that improve the water security of communities, towns, and cities in the Amazon in the face of the effects of climate change or other natural hazards. |  |

Source: funding proposal, annex 2, figure 78 (p. 215)

- Whereas eligibility criteria, project-specific criteria, strategic objectives and project categories (use the figures above) are spelled out in annexes 2 to the funding proposal, it is not clear to us how these relate to each other or to the different types of activity of the different components of the programme. There is a similar elaboration in the Operations Manual (annex 21), which makes some distinction between the eligibility criteria and strategic objectives. The distinction between project categories (PC) and IEF funds that implements them is clear, but it would not be natural to apply this distinction to the other components of the programme, namely component 2.1 (focused on infrastructure and services for medium-sized cities), 2.2 (focused on community and EFA for medium to large communities) rather than the whole of component 3 (focused on small communities and rural areas). These are more about capacity development and would seem to be more similar to those of components 1 and 3.

- The main consideration about selection criteria and institutional set-up for the different components is that they should be consistent with the eligibility checks. These checks are fully translated into the implementation modalities of outputs 2.1 and 2.2. The IEF sees these outputs as a means to achieve its strategic objectives, which are more focused on influencing the living conditions of the people of the Amazon basin. In this regard, it is important that the selection criteria for PC 3.1 and 3.2 be consistent with the requirements of WSW systems. A narrow focus on construction or technology would risk compromising matters of governance, cost recovery, maintenance and equitable distribution of services within participating communities.

- In sum, the programme is effectively a pool of capital that sits at the IDB with specific use of proceeds for projects in the Amazon region to achieve its strategic objectives. The actual underlying intervention will be developed on a case-by-case basis with the relevant stakeholders and national partners. In essence, the programme is managed as a count of a managed account at the IDB and is funded by interested contributing nations. At some level, it strikes the IEF that round-tripping funds through the IDB to manage this account alongside other GEF contributions is suboptimal from an overall efficiency standpoint.

- The efficiency-and-effectiveness criterion is assessed as medium.


---
**Page 138**
---

## Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

Overall, the funding proposal is comprehensive and opens a range of credible pathways towards addressing climate change and climate resilience in the Amazon basin. Since the subprojects to implement the programme are not yet defined, full detail of what the programme will be is consequently not available at this time. It is believed, nevertheless, that the executing entity will pay close attention to details and provide information on the nature of subprojects.

The ITAP recommends that the Board approve this funding proposal.

To further increase the sustainability and positive impact of the programme, the ITAP suggests the following recommendations:

### Recommendation 1: Systematic use of qualitative data for project monitoring, learning and communication
Systematic use of qualitative data for project monitoring, learning and communication in a rich manner, as well as ecosystem monitoring, in order to provide evidence-based information on the effectiveness of the programme.

### Recommendation 2: Complementing selection criteria with logical reasoning arguments
As a complement to the selection criteria, add a logical reasoning argument that demonstrates how the proposed programme will contribute towards building resilience of specific communities to specific climate change risks in the Amazon basin; and/or provide evidence-based information on the effectiveness of the programme.

### Recommendation 3: Emphasize production of learning materials and sharing of knowledge
Emphasis should be placed on the development of tools and mechanisms that can be designed and operationalized to support EbS solutions.

### Recommendation 4: Enable earliest possible handover to relevant agency or organization
Enable as early as possible a handover from the executing entity to another agency or organization with the purpose of assuring the appropriateness, functioning (and continued) maintenance of installed systems and/or infrastructure.

### Recommendation 5: Add instructions in design guidelines for early warning systems under component 1
Ensure that all MHEWS subprojects include sufficient attention to the development of a robust communication plan and action protocols.

### Recommendation 6: Use opportunities across programme for working with local communities and Indigenous Peoples as project participants
For the proactive blending of social, cultural, economic and environmental aspects into the design and engineering; and

### Recommendation 7: Enhance clarity of subprojects' institutional set-up and different roles more clearly between locally (mostly loan-funded) subprojects under outputs 2.1 and 2.2 and the broader (mostly grant-funded) subprojects
In order to implement the overall remaining activities of the programme.

# Response from the accredited entity to the independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment (FP261)

## Proposal name:
Improving Climate Resilience by Increasing Water Security in the Amazon Basin

## Accredited entity:
Inter-American Development Bank

## Country(ies):
Bolivia (Plurinational State of), Brazil, Colombia, Ecuador, Peru, Suriname

Large

### Impact potential
Many thanks for your recommendations. The proposed interventions aim to strengthen the resilience of vulnerable communities and ecosystems in the Amazon basin against climate change impacts on water security and ecosystem services. The programme will directly benefit 1.2 million people living in vulnerable areas across six countries, including indigenous peoples.

While complexity in assessment and quantifying the benefits is noted, the Programme integrates a participatory approach to project design, implementation, monitoring, evaluation, and continuous learning, as reflected in the means of verification of the Results Framework. The programme also reflects the importance of the process of identification of origination and selection of each sub-project, aligned with the impact rationale described in the Programme Document. This is consistent with the IDB’s focus on climate change adaptation and resilience contributions as this is part of the IDB impact framework and its focus on resilience.

This will ensure that each sub-project aligns with program goals and clearly articulates its purpose and expected results.

### Paradigm shift potential
Many thanks for your feedback. We agree that the potential for knowledge generation and dissemination across the three components of the proposal is considerable, specifically considering the cross-cutting nature of the programme’s objectives, interventions, and Programme Component 3 aims to contribute improving the enabling environment for climate investment and planning at policy and institutional level; to mitigate and manage risks in a systematic manner which is a good practice towards appropriating and long-term sustainability, which was also highlighted by the IDB. We will continue to work with IDBF in setting up and operation other similar investments. The details of the timing and transferability of the programme’s lessons learned will be addressed through investment and tailored-made to institutional arrangements and governance mechanisms in place.

We also welcome the system of qualitative data for project monitoring as it is line with the information systems that are already in place at IDB, which allows us to track this type of investments within the AE portfolio, when and if the sub-project impacts ecosystems.

# Sustainable development potential

Many thanks for your comments. We agree with the assessment of the programme that will significantly contribute to several of the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs).

Throughout the proposal, we placed significant emphasis on gender with the active involvement and participation of women in all aspects of the project implementation. It is worth to clarify that the training program is not exclusively designed for women; however, it has been developed from a gender perspective, thereby mitigating the risk of perpetuating unequal gender relations.

Furthermore, sanitation and hygiene are regarded as shared responsibilities among all genders.


## Needs of the recipient

Many thanks for your recommendation. As stated in the Funding Proposal, throughout the Programme's life cycle, an ongoing relationship with the Indigenous Peoples under the area of influence will be maintained through a variety of mechanisms including but not limited to: the provision of information, dialogue and meaningful consultation in a socio-cultural appropriate manner, respecting their governance structures, languages, and their rights.

This will ensure that the programme is implemented in a culturally sensitive way. The execution as well as through the compliance of the Environmental and social Framework of the programme with the donor's requirements will be ensured by the AE. In addition, where applicable, Free, Prior and Informed Consent (FPIC) of the affected Indigenous Peoples will be carried out when deemed necessary. It is worth mention that subjects will continuously feed and update the AE on their progress in terms of capacity building and other components with regards to the proactively engagement of IPs, not solely focusing on community-based inputs.


## Country ownership

Thanks for this feedback; the AE reckons that Indigenous peoples play a critical role in the Amazon and in this programme and recognises the invaluable contributions of ancestral knowledge, which views land, water, and ecosystems as interconnected systems and will leverage these values to ensure the success of the project. The AE has also been involved throughout the proposal. In this line, the Programme will indeed work proactively together with local communities and Indigenous peoples in the design and implementation of projects, particularly regarding the interventions under output 2.1 for small communities. It is foreseen that local communities and Indigenous peoples will be involved not only in sharing their traditional knowledge in the design of the solutions but also engaging with communities in participatory cultural assessments.


## Efficiency and effectiveness

Thanks for your comments and recommendation. While the programme has established selection criteria for subprojects that apply for all the programme -mainly Component 2-, those


---
**Page 141**
---

that will be part of broader activities (mostly grant-funded) must also respond to the description of activities of each of those sub-components.

The AE project team also reckons the critical role that governance, cost recovery, maintenance and equitable distribution of services play vis a vis the focus on construction and technology. The AE has made recommendation R7 on this regard and will be included during programme implementation.

The AE also recognises that each institutional set-up could be different depending on the project design and activities to be implemented. While general guidelines for institutional set-ups have been described in the Operation Manual of the programme, each specific subproject will need a tailored institutional set-up and will be related to the specific needs and activities comprised in each subproject.

Finally, we acknowledge ITAP's view regarding the design of programs which differs from IIFB experience. This does not mean that any experience is wrong or right.


Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel:

The ITAP thanks ITAP for the constructive comments and recommendations received on the Program, which are aligned with its objectives. Many of the recommendations received are already reflected in the current design of the programme. The remaining ones will be spelled out in this programmatic phase. We will make sure that ITAP's recommendations will be considered, strengthening even further a very impactful program for the Amazon.

# 1. INTRODUCTION

More than 34 million people, including 420 indigenous peoples, live in the Amazon Region and depend on nature for food security, livelihoods, and shelter. The average percentage of Amazonian women varies between 48.3% and 49.7% of the population and differs according to different ethnic groups that are identified in each country. Two-thirds of the Amazonian population are Brazilian, and more than half live in Brazil.

In the Amazon region, women play an essential role in territorial management and local forms of environmental conservation practices within indigenous, peasant, and Afro-descendant people. This is mainly related to their participation in the use of natural resources for food security, livelihoods, and care of the environment. Moreover, women in the Amazon region often actively participate in different environmental care practices and sustainable use of natural resources like productive activities in agriculture, livestock, and fisheries.

Amid the diversity of the Amazon region, women and men participate in different ways in decision-making regarding the management of natural resources and have different levels of access and control over these resources as productive assets. These differences also generate inequalities in the vulnerability and resilience that women and men have in the face of environmental risks.

This Gender Assessment aims to provide an overview of the gender situation in the countries that are included in the program "Improving Climate Resilience by Increasing Water Security in the Amazon Basin" (hereinafter referred to as the Project). The assessment will also identify opportunities for advancing gender equality, women's empowerment and the development of identity of indigenous peoples, the inclusion of persons with disabilities, Afro-descendants, and the LGBTIQ+ population through the financial activities, to inform the development of the project’s gender action plan.

# 2. THE IDB APPROACH TO GENDER EQUALITY AND DIVERSITY

The IDB is committed to promoting gender equality and empowerment, which, in addition to their intrinsic value, have been shown to contribute to economic growth, development effectiveness, and improving social inclusion and equity. The IDB’s Gender Equality Policy (hereinafter referred to as the Policy) gender equality means that women/girls and men/boys enjoy the same conditions and opportunities to obtain access to resources and services in all areas of life. The IDB’s Gender Equality Policy recognizes that pursuing equality requires actions aimed at equity, which implies providing and distributing benefits and/or resources in a way that narrows existing gaps. The understanding of gender equality is also based on the premise that rights and opportunities are not affected by diverse gender identities and gender expression.

Also, the IDB is committed to equal access to opportunities for Diversity and Population Groups (hereinafter referred to as DPGs). The Bank’s Gender Equality Policy recognizes that face discrimination based on their collective identity: (i) indigenous peoples; (ii) Afro-descendants; (iii) persons with disabilities; and (iv) people who identify as lesbian, gay, bisexual, transgender/transsexual or other sexual orientation minorities. This is referred to as Gender Diversity.

The IDB supports Gender & Diversity policies in the region through its operations, technical assistance and through the generation of data and evidence.


---
**Page 145**
---

# Amazon Population and Gender Diversity Aspects

The Amazon Region has a population of about 34 million people, of which approximately 2.2 million people are indigenous (approximately 4.6% of the Amazonic population) [ACHIR, 2019; RAISG, 2020]. The document discusses how many indigenous peoples live in this region: some sources, such as the CTCA (2018:12), indicate that there are approximately 420, while others, such as the Geodetic Socio-Environmental Information Network (RAISG, 2020) registers a total of 410. Similarly, it is not known how many indigenous women live in this region; some sources estimate that they number around two hundred [ACHIR, 2019], while others indicate there are more than four hundred [RAISG (2020)].

The common populations that live in the region are distributed as follows: 45% peasant, 14% Afro-descendant, 3% colons, 1%невротиков, and 1% fisherfolk (Paranámetro Nacional - REPARM, 2019). These proportions vary from country to country. For example, in the Ecuadorian Amazon region, mestizos represent 58%, followed by indigenous people at 34%, Afro-Ecuadorians with 3.8%, as Afro-Ecuadorian; and the rest in other categories such as black, mulatto, and montubio (INEC, 2016). In Colombia, 79% of Amazonian women identify themselves as mestizas, while only 45% identify with any ethnic group and belong to peasant communities; meanwhile, the indigenous population slightly exceeds 23%, and people who identify as black or mulatto are over 2% [DANE, 2019].

The division of the Amazonian population by sex has not been accurately identified. However, based on the latest data from the INEC (2016) in Ecuador, mestizas represent 58%, followed by indigenous people in the Amazon region - Bolivia, Colombia, Ecuador, and Peru – the average percentage of Amazonian women is 47%. In Brazil, 39% of Amazonian women identify as mestizas, while only 20% identify with any ethnic group that are identified in each country. In Colombia, 307,280 Amazonian women do not self-identify with an ethnic group; 92,200 do not identify as indigenous and 9,427 as Black, Mulato, Afro-Ecuadorians, or Afro-Colombians. Meanwhile, 15% of Amazonian women in Colombia identify as mestizas (210,462) and indigenous (121,463) [INEC, 2016]. Finally, in Peru, 735,597 Amazonian women do not self-identify with any ethnic group; 66.09% are native or indigenous to the Amazon and 38.109 as Quechua (INEI, 2017).


---
**Page 146**
---

Most of the population is Brazilian, accounting for more than 70% of the total population. Such population is followed by that of Peru and Bolivia with an estimated 15%, and 4% of the total Amazonian population (UCLG, 2019). However, other important municipalities are Manaus and Belém in Brazil, with about 2 million inhabitants each. In addition, there are other intermediate cities such as Iquitos (Peru) and Tumbes (Peru), where the population ranges from 50,000 to 80,000 people (INEI, 2017; National University of San Agustín).

In general, there are several characteristics of gender relations in the Amazon, however, each of these varies greatly depending on the social and environmental context from specific Amazonian social groups or ethnicities. These include: (i) patriarchal structures that are reinforced by religious institutions and other social groups; (ii) patriarchal cultural values among some indigenous groups assume that men represent the family and community in public arenas, leaving women without independent autonomy to make decisions about their lives and communities; (iii) gender roles are often rigidly defined; (iv) the division of labor by gender is often linked to different physical spaces and areas of activity, for example, women’s work tends to be more associated with domestic activities such as horticultural gardens, swidden gardens, including livestock, agroforestry and non-timber forest products while men dominate hunting, fishing, and the extraction of timber; (v) in contrast to the situation among non-indigenous communities, women’s arenas are often more limited, and men typically dominate agriculture; (vi) women’s productive work is often invisible to markets and outsiders due to its association with domestic activities.

The physical isolation of many forest communities makes women's collective action more difficult, and limits access to social services. However, the increasing importance of non-timber forest products in local economies and forest management have influenced community and gender dynamics, often creating or exacerbating inequalities between men and women. Women’s roles as producers of non-timber forest products and extractors of non-timber forest products and broader forest management tasks (vii) over the past three decades. Amazonian women have found diverse ways to organize in support of their community struggles for land and territory, including through the creation of women's organizations, cooperatives, and credit unions projects, and to gradually build their skills and leadership within broader community and public arenas.

Nevertheless, indigenous peoples are at the forefront of urban/rural ethnicity and ethnic condition, with indigenous peoples being the ones furthest behind.


### 4. LEGAL, REGULATORY, AND INSTITUTIONAL FRAMEWORK

The seven countries considered under this study have ratified or acceded to the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) as an optional protocol. They also have ratified the Inter-American Convention on the Prevention, Punishment, and Eradication of


---
**Page 147**
---

# Violence against Women (Convention of Belém Do Para)

Their constitutions address equality before the law and no one can be discriminated against on sex. Most countries have performed a normative reform to establish an adequate framework for the protection of women's norms; advances still face a deeply patriarchal and androcentric culture that is reflected in many social and institutional practices.

## A. BOLIVIA

The constitution of Bolivia regulates gender equality and non-discrimination in its articles 8, 11, 14, 15, and 20. It also establishes the right to equal remuneration for work of equal value with respect to norms in the General Labor Law regarding the percentage of women that companies can employ (not exceeding 30% of total employment), and the working hours for women (maximum of 40 hours per week, compared to 48 hours for men) (Art. 15).

The Constitution establishes shared responsibility in household tasks for both spouses or partners (Art. 64), specifies that all its members have equal rights, obligations, and opportunities (Art. 62), and recognizes the right of women to receive remuneration for their work performed outside the home, including in public accounts (Art. 338). Remunerated domestic work is regulated by Law No. 2,450, Supreme Decree No. 224, and Supreme Decree No. 4,589.

In Bolivia, the Plurinational Service for Justice and Departialization (SEPUDI) is a decentralized institution created in 2019 by the Ministry of Justice and Institutional Transparency. Its purpose is to monitor, follow up, and evaluate the implementation of public policies toward women's rights, promote their effective exercise of women's rights, and promote the eradication of all forms of violence and discrimination against women.

B. BRAZIL

The 1988 Constitution symbolizes the legal framework for the re-democratization of Brazil. For the formulation of the 1988 Constitution, popular participation was ensured. Regarding women's participation, the Charter of Brazilian Women to the Constituents stands out, which compiled the main demands of the movement.

The 1988 Constitution stands out also, involving by inaugurating its Text bringing fundamental rights, and already in its Article 5, expressly establishes equality between men and women in general. Another important feature is that it abolished several provisions that placed women in an inferior role within the family entity. Thus, the following constitutional provisions stand out: that the rights and duties relating to the conjugal society are exercised by both spouses; that the husband and wife have equal authority over their children in the person of each of its members, creating mechanisms to curb violence in the context of their relationships; or that 1990 text expressly introduced the prohibition of discrimination in the labor market on the grounds of gender or marital status.


---
**Page 150**
---

The Ministry of Women, Family and Human Rights (former Ministry of Human Rights(2016–2018), and Secretariat for Human Rights of the Presidency of the Republic (1997-2015) is an office attached to the Presidency of Brazil. Its purpose is to implement, promote, and protect human rights, civic rights, and the rights of women.

National Secretariat of Policies for Women (SNPMF) is linked to the Ministry of Women, Family and Human Rights and its main objective is to promote equality between men and women and combat all forms of prejudice and discrimination inherent from a patriarchal and exclusionary society. Since its creation in 2004, it has been responsible for creating and implementing national policies on gender, through the valorization of women and their inclusion in the country's social, economic, political, and cultural life.

The SNPMF is part of the Ministry of Women, Family, and Human Rights and is the National Secretariat of Policies for Women in the organizational structure of the Ministry of Women, Family, and Human Rights.


In 2004, The National Plan of Policies for Women was created to combat gender discrimination and to construct national policies for an equal gender perspective. These plans are renewed periodically. Contributes to the strengthening and institutionalization of the Policy National Convention for Women (CNPMF) as a permanent forum for dialogue between the State and civil society. As a national plan, it reaffirms the guiding principles of the National Policy for Women: (i) women's autonomy in all dimensions life; (ii) search for effective equality between women and men, in all spheres of life; (iii) promotion of women's participation as full partners in decision-making at every level of the State; and universality of services and benefits offered by the State; (v) active participation of women in public life and politics; and (vi) promotion of gender equality through public policies.

Programs such as "Minha casa, minha vida" (My house, my life), created in 2009 to help people with low-income to buy their own houses, almost 80% of the beneficiaries are women;and the 'Bolso Família' (Family Packet), created in 2003, through which payments are transferred by the government to poor families, also has a significant role of women in social life. ### C. COLOMBIA

The 1991 Constitution establishes (i) the principle of non-discrimination for reasons of sex, race, national or family origin, religion, language, political or religious option (article13); (ii) equal rights and duties of men and women in all areas; (iii) the right to a family life that is considered destructive of their harmony and their article 42; (iv) the adequate and effective participation of women in economic, social, cultural, civil and political life; and (v) equal rights and opportunities for men and women and establishes that women may not be subjected to no kind of discrimination and that she will receive special assistance during pregnancy and after childbirth.

The Presidential Council for the Equity of Women, dependent on the Vice Presidency of the Republic, has assigned the following functions among others: (i) designing policies, plans, programs, projects, and necessary provisions designed to promote equality generation and empowerment for women; (ii) negotiating and concluding agreements with international organizations and treaties, international agreements and accords that relate to gender equity for women and (iii) establishing a public policy on the rights of women in Colombia. The Council works with government sector, women's organizations, international organizations, organizations from civil society (ONG, universities and research centers), for the implementation of a public gender policy.

In the last three decades, national governments have developed different policies for women: In 1992, the Government established the National Plan for Women; in 1994, the Participation and Equality Act on Women's Policy; in 1999, the Plan for Equal Opportunities for Women; in 2003, the Women Peace Building Plan; and in 2007, the National Plan for Gender Equity. The implementation of these policies, since then has been led by the entities responsible for the issue of gender equity. Table 4.3 shows the laws with an impact on the rights of women in Colombia.

### D. ECUADOR

The 2008 Constitution acquires a series of commitments to achieve gender equality, including: promotion of parity in the appointment of public positions, elimination of violence in the private and public sphere against women, formulation and execution of policies to achieve equity of men and women, guaranteeing equal access to property and in decision-making in the family. It also guarantees different forms of participation, recognition and productive work of labor unpaid self- support and human care that takes place in homes, assurance of actions and services for sexual and reproductive health and protection under the law of gender equality.

As of the promulgation of 2008 Constitution, different policies and programs oriented by the State are oriented towards the effective enjoyment of rights to achieve equity between women and men; the gender approach is incorporated in plans and programs and technical assistance is provided for its mainstreaming into public policies.

The National Council for Gender Equality was created in 1972 as the Department of Women. Later, in 1984, it became the National Office for Women. In 1986, it took the category of National Directorate for Women. Its main objective is to promote and ensure full equality of women in political, legal, psychological, economic, educational, ethical, cultural life and access to all conditions and participation within the Ministry of Social Welfare.

It becomes the National Council of Women CONAMIE with administrative, financial, and technical autonomy, which has a gender approach. Its specific objective was the incorporation of the gender approach in plans, programs, and projects, and provide advice and assistance technique for the promotion of equality between women and men within public organizations through the mainstreaming and implementation of Equal Opportunity Plans; its objectives are to promote equity and equal access of rights and rights of women and the LGBTI population.

The National Agenda for Women and LGBTI people 2018-2021 is a mandatory instrument of national policy. It was approved in the National Development Plan 2017-2021, it becomes the main strategy to mainstream the principle of equality among discrimination due to Gender, both for public management and for the empowerment of rights holders; a fundamental element of the entire legal system and the national political system. Code of Democracy or Organic Law on Elections and Political Organizations

Regulates the application of parity between men and women in the lists for multi-person elections. It establishes impediments to being candidates who violate the law against gender violence and not complying with the child support payments.

Organic Law of the Legislative Function

It transversally incorporates the focus on women's rights and gender equality, through the creation of the Legislative Technical Unit, whose objective is to ensure that all legislative acts are analyzed from a gender perspective. It also includes the establishment of the Specialized Commissions and the Plenary with, of a non-reporting on bills such as those that contain gender-sensitive language intrinsically standard and the gender impact of the suggested standards.

Organic Law of the Council of Citizen Participation and Social Control

Guarantees citizens, individually or collectively, equal rights, conditions and opportunities to participate, influencing decisions in the public life of the territory and society.

Organic Code of Territorial Organization, Autonomy and Decentralization

The Cantonal Council for the Protection of Rights will harness attributions for the formulation, mainstreaming, observance, monitoring and evaluation of municipal public policies for the protection of rights, articulated with the public policies office National Council for the Equality.

Organic Code of Planning and Public Finance

It establishes that coordination spaces will be established in the exercise of planning and public policy, in order to incorporate gender, ethnic-cultural, intergenerational and territorial diversity.

The Comprehensive Organic Criminal Code

Typifies three types of crimes of violence against women members of the family: nuclear; physical violence, psychological violence and sexual violence.

Children and Adolescents Code

Establishes protection mechanisms against mistreatment abuse, sexual exploitation, trafficking and loss of rights for children and adolescents. It stipulates violation rights, parental authority and food.

Work Code

It specifies the rights of working women and men, maternal/infant paternity leave, the right to equal remuneration, dismissals formally, the obligation to enroll in insurance for medical and social benefits.

Law on Violence against Women and the Family (Law 103)

It makes an explicit approach to violence against women and the family. It includes protection measures for victims and the prosecution of the perpetrator. It also establishes the right to physical and mental intergenerational sexual freedom of the woman and the members of her family.

Free Maternity and Child Care Law

Guarantees the right of women to free quality and warm health care, during pregnancy, childbirth and postpartum, access to sexual and reproductive health programs.

Organic Law of Public Service of 2010

Guarantees the equality of men and women in termination and appointment positions, in the selection processes and incorporation into the public service.

---

E. PERU

The 1993 Political Constitution of Peru establishes that no one should be discriminated based on origin, race, sex, language, religion, opinion, economic condition, or any other nature.

The mission of the Ministry of Women and Vulnerable Populations is to become an integrated, modern, and articulating body, contributing to overcoming poverty, inequality and exclusion, with a focus on comprehensive human development and equity and generating equal opportunities for men and women.

The General Directorate for Gender Equality and Non-Discrimination oversees compliance with national and sectoral policies for Gender Equality and Non-Discrimination at the national level within the scope of their competencies related to mitigation and prevention of gender-based violence.

The Gender and Climate Change Action Plan is a management instrument that seeks to guide the action of different entities of the Peruvian State within the framework of their competencies related to mitigation and adaptation to climate change, with the objective of contributing to equality between men and women in the following prioritized areas: Forests, Water Resources, Energy, Food Security, Solid Waste, Health, Education and Disaster Risk Management.

Table 4.8 National regulatory framework that impacts on women in Peru

| Laws (year) | Description |
| --- | --- |
| Law No. 28983 (2007) | Law of Equal Opportunities between Women and Men. This norm establishes that the State has the duty to adopt all the necessary measures to promote and ensure equality between men and women, avoiding any form of discrimination (art. 4). |
| Supreme Decree No. 007-2007- PCM (2007) | Defines and establishes mandatory national policies. Equality of men and women, directly applies as indicators for each National Policy. |

# F. SURINAME

Suriname’s Constitution of 1987 with Amendments through 1992 states that (i) no one shall be discriminated against on the grounds of birth, sex, race, language, religion, origin, education, political beliefs or social status; (ii) every woman has a right to health care before and after pregnancy for minors, disabledpersons and for those who are engaged in work which is harmful to their health. The Constitution also states that (iii) every woman shall be entitled to paid maternity leave, and (iv) all employees have independence of age, sex, race, nationality, religion or political opinion; the right to a remuneration for their work corresponding to quality and quantity.

The Bureau for Gender Policy was established in 1987 and became officially operational in 1998. In 2015 the name of the bureau was changed to the Bureau of Gender Affairs (BGA). The goal of the BGA is to develop, implement, coordinate, monitor, evaluate and disseminate a national gender policy that will create a balanced development with equal opportunities for men and women.

Since it was set up, the Bureau has developed two Integrated Gender Plans of Action (IGAP 2000- 2005 and IGAP 2016-2021) and a Gender Focus Point in various ministries as part of the government’s Gender Management System.

Suriname's Multi-Year Development Plan 2001-2005 was led by a rights-based approach to development and identified gender as a cross-cutting issue across all programs, projects, and activities. The Ministry of Home Affairs, particularly the BGA, is responsible for the implementation and monitoring of CEDAW. Since then it has worked or adjusted the following national legislation since the ratification of the CEDAW:

- Revision of the Criminal Code regarding the trade in women and minors in 2006.


---
**Page 157**
---

- Revision of the Criminal Code regarding sexual crimes in 2009.
- Bill against Stalking in 2012: according to this law preventive measures can be taken by the public prosecutor to protect a possible victim.
- Adoption of the law on Minimum Health Insurance (2014).
- Adoption of the law on Minimum Hourly Wage (2014).
- Adoption of the law on General Pension (2014).
- ILO Convention concerning Equal Remuneration for Men and Women Workers of Equal Value (Equal Remuneration Convention), 1951 no. 100, Geneva, 01 June 1951 (2016): equal pay for men and women.
- Law approving the accession of the Republic of Suriname to the ILO Convention concerning Equal Remuneration for Men and Women Workers of Equal Value (Equal Remuneration Convention), 1951 no. 100, Geneva, 01 June 1951 (2016): equal wage for men and women.
- Law approving accession of the Republic of Suriname to the Convention concerning Discrimination in Respect of Employment and Occupation (Discrimination Employment and Occupation) Convention, 1958 no. 111, Geneva, 25 June 1958 (2016): policy for equal opportunities to prevent discrimination concerning labor.
- Law on Labor Migration (2016).
- At present, a Law on Sexual Harassment and a bill on paid maternity leave are on draft.

## 5. GENERAL AND SPECIFIC SECTOR GAPS

To establish comparable indicators among the countries under scrutiny in this report, an analysis of the gender situation in the study area is presented below. This analysis is based on data extracted from the Global Gender Gap Report (2023), which focuses on education attainment, economic participation and employment, health status, political empowerment, and health (Table 5.1).

Table 5.1 shows that none of the countries has reached parity (index = 1), largely attributable to indicators related to economic participation and political empowerment. However, indicators related to educational attainment are closer to parity than those in other areas such as women's participation in technical and professional areas. Likewise, parity is observed in indicators related to health and survival.

### Education and Employment

Based on data extracted from the Global Gender Gap Report (2023), the detailed study of the education attainment data (Fig. 5.1) highlights that in all the countries under study female and male participation at primary and secondary levels are mostly the same. At the tertiary level, women's participation is higher than men's.

[1] WEF (2023): Global Gender Gap Report 2023. Geneva: Sulza Autor. The Global Gender Gap Index has been introduced by the World Economic Forum since 2006 to define a framework to measure gender equality in countries and track progress over time. It is based on four pillars: (1) Educational Attainment, (2) Opportunity and Economic Participation, (3) Political Empowerment, and (4) Health and Survival. Its Political Empowerment pillar provides indexes for the following effective comparisons between regions and income groups and series: 0 means parity and 1 represents 100% inequality.

Child labor rates are high in the Amazon region. The main causes are found in family poverty and their need to contribute to the family economy. A greater proportion of married and maternity of women at an early age is greater than in the non-Amazonian population. Girls and adolescents are the most affected by child labor, especially those who live in rural areas. Child labor is often hidden during their childhood and is heralded and usually has consequences in future stages of their lives associated with low educational levels and poor health (UN Women, UNFPA & UNESCO, 2018). In Bolivia, 16% of children between 10 and 14 years old and more than 40% between 15 and 17 years old are already working (2010). Brazil present similar levels: around 30% of children aged 10 to 14 years work, while in Peru this figure is close to 60% for 17-year-olds who work (2010). The incidence of child labor is higher among indigenous and Afro-descendant children; this difference presents its widest gaps in Bolivia, Brazil, and Peru (CEPAL, 2022)### Political representation.

A similar trend is observed in the participation of women in the communal water boards of the countries under study. An exhaustive analysis was carried out by Matos et al (2021) [1] on the profile of the members of inter-municipal based water boards, which shows that the participation of women in decision-making positions is very low while in administrative and support positions (assistant) they reach values above 50% (Fig. 18).

Fig. 5.10 Positions on boards of directors of interstate river basin committees in Brazil.

In Peru, women hold 26.8% of posts on water boards, and their representation in decision-making roles stands at a mere 7%. A 2020 study conducted by the Ministry of Housing, Construction, and Sanitation of Peru revealed compelling evidence indicating that increased female participation in water committees enhanced overall management effectiveness. Specifically, when women occupy key positions such as chairperson or vice-chairperson, they contribute to various operational aspects, including streamlined administrative processes, enhanced monthly fee collection, better monitoring of financial sustainability, and improved cleaning and disinfection protocols utilizing chlorine.

Despite these advancements, there were no observable enhancements in the maintenance and operation of water systems, which could potentially impact service continuity. These findings underscore the need for further research into how women's participation can be maximized across various domains, thereby empowering them to contribute more effectively to the management of water resources.

Regarding intimate partner violence, violence against women is the one with the most comparable statistics among countries. This indicates that 64% of women in Bolivia have ever had a partner and who have ever suffered physical and/or sexual violence by their partner.

Bolivia has the highest percentage (64.10%), followed by Ecuador and Colombia (around 37%).
