In Brazil, between 2016 and 2021, there was an increase in the femicide rate of over 44%. In the state of Amazonas, out of all the women intentionally murdered by another person, more than one in five cases were of femicides.

In Ecuador, 1 in 4 women have experienced sexual violence, yet psychological violence is the most recurrent form of gender violence, occurring in 53.9% of cases. Of all women who have experienced physical violence, 87.3% have experienced it within their intimate partner relationships. Additionally, 6 out of 10 women report having been victims of sexual violence and/or psychological violence; with a higher percentage among indigenous women (68.7%) and Afro-Ecuadorian women (66.7%).

In Peru, official reports indicate that 7 out of 15 adult women have been victims of psychological, physical or sexual violence at some point in their lives. In the year 2020, due to the totalization caused by the pandemic, gender-based violence increased from 14,049 cases in March to 24,990 cases in December.

In Colombia, the National Institute of Legal Medicine and Forensic Sciences reported 27,594 cases of violence against girls and adolescent women between 2015 and 2019. In 2021, 78 women were murdered. Four out of ten murders of women are committed by partners, ex-partners, family members, or acquaintances. The National Institute of Legal Medicine and Forensic Sciences reported an increase in gender-based violence in 2020 (PIN): 330,000 of them, predominantly indigenous and Afro-descendant, living in the Pacific region of the country[1].

Access to Finance,

Women represent more than 40% of the economically active population in LAC. Only 17% of them are entrepreneurs in the formal economy. The main barriers they encounter to forming formal companies are (a) difficulties in leaving the informal sector (more than 60% of LAC women work in this sector)[2] or going beyond their own household, reducing their social position[3]; (b) gender barriers that prevent them from working long hours outside the home; (c) ignorance about market and existing opportunities; (d) reduced levels of financial education; (e) lack of female roles to follow in business; (f) lack of support networks; and (g) difficulties in accessing financing without legal ownership or guarantee[4].

According to a study of 35 entrepreneurs in 15 countries in LAC, a high number of women entrepreneurs in the region feel that they have been discriminated against because of their gender (27% versus 4%) for their business. In addition, women entrepreneurs are more likely than men to report having had the second highest rate of bankruptcies of companies run by women worldwide. While most male entrepreneurs cited lack of capital as a barrier to starting and running businesses[5], women entrepreneurs point to the difficulty of obtaining financing as the main impediment to continuing with their businesses. The credit gap in LAC is $300 for women-led micro businesses and $550 for women-led small businesses.

The Global Banking Alliance ensures that women clients show a stronger saving behavior (16% more likely than men to save for futures), with lower risk (on average, they are more likely to repay loans), with higher returns on investment and better credit scores. Microfinance intermediaries that implement programs that effectively meet the needs of women see excellent financial returns.

Access to land and women's territorial care.
Although in the region there are some large and medium urban centers such as Manaus, Belém or Iquitos, the territory is eminently rural. In rural areas, the predominant forms of land management are protected natural areas, indigenous and community territories, and private properties including different production systems (agriculture, livestock, forestry). Women have a significant role in managing these lands among men and women, as it is usually Amazonian men who have ownership and administration over the land. In addition, women's access to land tenure is limited: only 10% of rural women own their land, while 85% of maleholders own it (PROAMAZONIA, 2019[6]). Likewise, in Colombia, women's access to land tenure is precarious and subject to the same rights and shared tenure with partners or social groups (Santos et al., 2013[7]; Zorio, 2011[8]).

Different social, economic, legal, and cultural factors can explain this inequality in land tenure. For instance, Amazonian women have less access to state identification or visibility processes that allow them to obtain titles for their lands (Villar, 2017[9]). In addition, there are 58% of Amazonian women who speak a native language and 7.2% of those who speak Spanish do not even have a National Identification Document (Villar, 2017[9]). In addition, although there is a shared tenure within collective property systems, it is usually Amazonian men who assume mostly the custody and administration of the lands (PROAMAZONIA, 2019).

However, although Amazonian women do not have ownership over land wealths, they do play an essential role in managing these resources and local forest and water services, as well as their relation with Indigenous, Peasant, and Afro-descendant peoples. This is mainly because women oversee home and family care, which also often encompass the care of the natural environment. In fact, the PROAMAZONIA (2019[6]) report states that in the case of indigenous communities, for example, while on average 23 hours a week to reproductive work, almost 17 longer hours than men. Although this gap has not been measured in Afro-descendant and peasant women, it has been found that usually all Amazonian women spend much more time on this type of work than men.

In addition, women are also active in the productive activities of agriculture, livestock, forestry, and fisheries in which different forms of environmental care and sustainable use of natural resources are exercised. In the case of indigenous populations, for example, women represent 50% of the agricultural workforce (in seven percentage points less than men, approximately (PROAMAZONIA, op.cit.). In the case of the indigenous population, women's participation in productive activities is also related to their role as managers and decision-makers within the family. Women are usually responsible for managing food production scenarios as sceneries of family agricultural production that allow subsistence, the conservation of diversity and, in many cases, the generation of economic surpluses for the home (PROAMAZONIA, op.cit.). In the case of productive activities of peasant or mestizo families, women have a high participatory production role.

activities but also in decision-making and resource management.

Women are more vulnerable to the impacts of climate change. Some research argued that this is the case because they have fewer economic resources to mitigate impacts, for example productive systems based on natural resources such as forests, water, and soil. Women's livelihoods and environmental care activities create a strong dependence on natural resources so any change in such resources has tremendous effects on their livelihoods as well as on their personal and social lives. More importantly, "In rural areas women have direct access to forest resources of the environment, on greater soil wealth, on greater clean water, for greater and better forest management, for greater and better seed production, for greater food security, for greater income generation, for greater health care" (Montalvo & Natale, 2019; ONU Mujeres, 2019). In addition, women face increased risks of contagion of tropical diseases, such as malaria, dengue fever and chikungunya. Women are more vulnerable to these diseases because they have less access to health services and that women are differently affected by deforestation. This is because, while men own land and can take advantage of tree felling for timber sales or the expansion of productive activities, deforestation disrupts the fundamental natural resources most women use to ensure the livelihoods of their families (Villar, 2013).

That is why, current research has emphasized the importance of generating a gender perspective in forest management, biodiversity conservation, climate change mitigation and sustainable development (OTCA, 2019; PROAmazonía, 2019 & ONU Mujeres, 2019. Our Future Forests Amazonia Verde, 2021). There is a growing call to foster greater participation of women in the management of protected areas and natural resources. In the Amazon region, women are the main actors in land use and occupy more than 47% of the Amazonian territory. In fact, in the Amazon, there are about 500 protected areas that cover approximately 12 million hectares (Montalvo & Natale, 2019; ONU Mujeres, 2019; RASG, 2020). The country with the highest coverage of protected areas is Brazil with more than 65% of its territory covered by protected areas. In Peru and Colombia, this percentage is lower according to the Amazon Network of Georeferenced Socio-Environmental Information, there are two areas (of direct and indirect use) that could benefit from a more substantial inclusion of women within government policies.

Areas of direct use in which natural resources are conserved within the framework of management plans that regulate and control the different uses. These areas can occupy just under 40% of the Amazon region, extension andmore than 50% of the Protected Natural Areas of the Amazon (RASG, 2020).

Areas of indirect use where biodiversity protection is promoted in parallel with research, education and sustainable development activities and are generally not allowed. It is estimated that these areas can occupy about 12% of the Land Average of the Amazon and makeup just over 48% of the Protected Natural Areas of the region.

This is particularly relevant because there are 2,275 indigenous territories covering more than

---
**Page 172**
---

204,003 km² of the Amazon (RAISG, 2020). The land tenure categories of these territories may vary between officially recognized traditional occupation and indigenous lands. Temporal use and occupancy are official recognition: Indigenous Reserve or Intangible Zone for (PROACI), Indigenous Reserve Proposal (RAISG, 2020). The countries with the largest number of indigenous territories are Brazil (18) and Peru (15), followed by Colombia (9) and Ecuador (7), with an area of 262,912 km². Most of these indigenous territories are officially recognized and occupy 21.9% of the coverage of the entire Amazon (RAISG, 2017). Different studies have highlighted that the role of indigenous peoples in land management is low or non-existent; most of them are mainly responsible for the administration of resources (PROAMAZONIA, 2018).

However, there are already multiple organizations that have strong representation and viability of women in their leadership. For example, the Confederation of Indigenous Women of the Brazilian Amazon (COIBA) and the Articulation of Indigenous Peoples Brazil (APIB) have a high representation of women promoting indigenous rights and environmental sustainability (Lima and Vieira, 2019). Amazonian women (both indigenous and non-indigenous) are also increasingly involved in decision-making processes at different protected areas and indigenous or community territories because they have led many of the care activities related to water and sanitation. This is a result of their traditional knowledge and practices (Lima and Vieira, 2019). Many of these processes respond to the emergence of bioeconomy strategies to use native species for economic development in the Amazon region (Lima and Vieira, 2019; RAISG, 2020). Thus, these areas have great potential to mitigate the negative consequences of contemporary environmental degradation and ensure the conservation of the biological diversity of the Amazon.

In addition, indigenous territories are also characterized by their high level of protection. More than 75% of Protected Natural Areas and Indigenous Territories are free from environmental degradation related in the last decade (RAISG, 2019). This is due to the fact that these areas have a low population density. There are even multiple studies that point to the relationship between the delineation of these territories and the potential impact on deforestation, which favors the maintenance of ecosystem services crucial for bioeconomy such as carbon capture, biodiversity protection, and conservation of hydrological systems (RAISG, 2020).

### Gender and diverse populations' challenges related to water and sanitation.

Globally, 1.8 billion people live in households without water supply. Women and girls aged 15 and older are primarily responsible for water collection in 7 out of 10 such households, compared with 3 in 10 households where men or boys collect the water (WHO/UNICEF, 2019). In many cases, women and girls make longer journeys to collect it, losing time in education, work, and other activities. This is especially true for indigenous populations living in remote areas. However, women and girls are disproportionately affected by the lack of access to basic water, sanitation, and hygiene services due to their needs during periods of increased vulnerability to infection around menstruation and pregnancy.

Without gender equality and shifts in patriarchal culture, sustainable water, sanitation, and hygiene (WASH) outcomes are challenging to secure; women's voices are likely to remain unheard, and initiatives that do not consider the specific needs of women will be less effective. Women are responsible for most household WASH activities; strategic improvements in WASH can also be a pathway towards broader gender equality (Willettts et al., 2013). Multiple established connections link access to safely managed water services with improved health and well-being, especially among women.

In terms of accessing drinking water less than 40% of the population in the Amazon region have access to drinking water. (i) The Amazon region of Ecuador presents the greatest gaps compared to the rest of the country.

The effects of climate change on women and diverse populations.

The impacts of climate change reverberate deeply, affecting both women and diverse populations in unique and profound ways, with indigenous communities often bearing disproportionate burdens. According to the Living Amazon Report (2022), the Amazon rainforest, often referred to as "the Living Planet," is home to more than 400 million people belonging to over 500 different groups. These communities rely on the forest for food, medicine, shelter, and cultural practices.

However, climate change exacerbates these impacts by altering weather patterns and increasing the frequency of extreme events such as floods and droughts. This can lead to changes in ecosystems and livelihoods, posing significant challenges for women and indigenous rural communities who bear the primary responsibility for securing water, food, and fuel for their families (FAO, 2017**).

Indigenous peoples face distinct challenges stemming from their close relationship with the land and their traditional lifestyles. Climate change compounds existing vulnerabilities, threatening cultural heritage and biodiversity. The frequency of extreme weather events can disrupt agricultural practices and force communities to relocate from ancestral territories (U.S. Climate Resilience Toolkit).

According to the Living Amazon Report 2022**, the Amazon rainforest is home to more than 400 million people belonging to over 500 different groups. These communities rely on the forest for food, medicine, shelter, and cultural practices.

Moreover, indigenous communities in the Amazon are often marginalized and lack access to essential services such as healthcare, education, and clean water. As climate change intensifies, these disparities become more pronounced, further exacerbating their vulnerability (IPCC, 2014; Climate Change 2014: Synthesis Report).

According to Taibah-Wright's research (2023), it is crucial to consider the various impacts of climate change based on gender when identifying necessary adaptations for the health sector. Gender-defined occupations and social attitudes often shape how climate change affects health (WHO, 2018).


---
**Page 174**
---

For example, heatwaves and rising temperatures disproportionately affect the health of male-dominated communities. Conversely, in rural regions, women experience heightened food insecurity due to traditional gender roles that often place them lower than men in terms of education, income, and social status compared to men (Romano et al., 2021). Governments can address inequalities in climate change adaptation by integrating gender perspectives into sectoral policy and planning (WHO, 2018). An initial step involves identifying gender disparities through enhancing the availability of health data disaggregated by gender (Romano et al., 2021).

Moreover, climate change exacerbates health risks for women and diverse populations in the Amazon, including indigenous peoples. The loss of livelihoods, biodiversity loss, and social unrest increases the incidence of injuries, diseases, and maternal deaths (UNFPA, 2019). Displacement due to environmental degradation can lead to increased vulnerability among diverse populations to heightened risks of exploitation, human trafficking, and gender-based violence as they struggle to rebuild their lives in unfamiliar and often precarious environments (UNCHR, 2020).

Climate change also worsens mental health among indigenous peoples, as noted in Talbot-Wright's book (Talbot-Wright et al., 2019). The loss of traditional lands, hunting grounds, and the loss of livelihoods, biodiversity loss, and social unrest can cause a range of mental issues, including anxiety, depression, post-traumatic stress disorder, suicidal tendencies, aggression, and intimate partner violence, often against women (IPCC, 2022).

In the face of these multifaceted challenges, it is imperative to recognize the invaluable knowledge and resilience of indigenous women and diverse populations in the Amazon. Empowering these communities through participatory approaches and ensuring their voices are heard can inform support for sustainable adaptation strategies crucial for building resilience and fostering equitable outcomes in the face of climate change. By centering on the voices and experiences of those most affected, we can work towards a more just and sustainable future for all inhabitants of the Amazon basin.

Gender and Diversity Action Plan

The Gender and Diversity Action Plan provides a gender and social inclusion-specific and time-bound framework to operationalize relevant gender and diversity activities based on the biodiversity conservation priorities of the Amazon. The plan will be implemented through complementary activities with targets on gender and inclusion of Indigenous Peoples (IP), Afro-descendant communities, women, and persons with disabilities in the context of the Amazon.

These activities will primarily focus on integrating gender and diversity considerations throughout the Fund's activities and outputs. However, to effectively achieve this, it is crucial to include specific initiatives dedicated to mainstreaming gender and diversity. The activities will be framed around three main pillars: (i) capacity building; (ii) project implementation; and (iii) monitoring and evaluation.

To ensure the success of these activities, the Inter-American Development Bank (IDB) will fund a comparative study on gender equality in the Amazon region both inside and outside. This study will examine: (a) the challenges and barriers faced by women, LGBTQI+ indigenous peoples, Afro-descendants, persons with disabilities, and peasant communities regarding climate change adaptation and mitigation; and (b) the opportunities for increasing the participation of women in water-related roles such as collection, operation, and maintenance.

Furthermore, the IDB will ensure that all projects under its portfolio incorporate a mainstreaming approach. The IDB will undertake: (i) Gender analysis studies for each project, including an assessment of gender equality gaps and barriers to gender equality; (ii) Specific activities in project proposals; and (iii) Specific studies within relevant activities to ensure the gender and diversity mainstreaming.

Moreover, efforts will be made to ensure the active participation of women and indigenous, Afro-descendant LGBTQI+, and persons with disabilities populations in all training activities whenever possible. The IDB will also seek to include women and persons with disabilities who speak languages previously identified as reaching these communities effectively. Collaborative efforts between representatives of organizations of diverse groups will be pursued to ensure the proper implementation of gender activities. Lastly, project proposals will be adapted to accommodate persons with disabilities and the biodiversity population groups as Indigenous and Afro-descendant communities, incorporating cultural adaptations as necessary.

1 Some of the specific actions that will be considered to ensure women have access to capacity building activities are (i) encouraging women's participation by highlighting the importance of their participation; (ii) selecting venues in more locations in assemblies and events; (iii) providing childcare services for children under 6 years old, including those with disabilities; (iv) providing flexible schedules and provide when possible virtual workshops to allow women to participate from home. When feasible, family-friendly policies will be implemented by the IDB to ensure that women can participate in training activities; (v) ensuring that activities are relevant and accessible to women from different backgrounds and skill levels and provide materials and facilitators who can communicate effectively with them; (vi) providing opportunities for women to share their experiences and challenges, learning from women's participants to further understand their needs and challenges and using the feedback to improve the design and delivery of activities to become more inclusive and accessible.

# Monitoring and Evaluation

The success of the Gender Action Plan within the Improving Climate Resilience by Increasing Water Security in the Amazon Basin Project hinges on a robust Monitoring and Evaluation (M&E) framework that systematically collects data to inform adaptive management strategies. These processes are designed to be dynamic, responsive, and participatory, ensuring that gender and social inclusivity remains a core focus throughout the project's lifecycle.

It is important to regularly assess the Gender Action Plan’s progress through gender-sensitive indicators, stakeholder feedback mechanisms, periodic reviews, and transparent reporting. Elements based on lessons learned and emerging gender and diversity considerations ensure the continued effectiveness of the plan. Specific actions may include:

1. **Gender sensitive indicators:** Analyze the gender sensitive indicators that are included in the gender action plan to gauge the effectiveness of gender mainstreaming efforts.
2. **Stakeholder feedback mechanisms:** Analyze feedback to identify specific challenges and opportunities for improvement.
3. **Periodic gender and diversity reviews:** Conduct periodic reviews specifically dedicated to assessing the progress made by the Gender Action Plan, project achievements, shortcomings in the implementation of gender-and-diversity specific initiatives and the overall responsiveness of project activities to emerging gender and diversity considerations. Use these findings as inputs into adaptive management strategies.
4. **Adaptive Management Strategies:** Utilize findings to inform adaptive management strategies that respond to emerging gender and diversity considerations. Implement timely adjustments to project activities, outputs, or outcomes in response to new information on gender and diversity issues. This adaptive approach ensures that the Gender Action Plan remains relevant, effective, and capable of addressing evolving challenges.
5. **Reporting Mechanisms:** Regularly report on the progress of gender and social inclusivity efforts through transparent and accessible channels. Disseminate information on gender and diversity considerations to ensure that all stakeholders are informed and engaged. Ensure that reporting mechanisms are tailored to reach diverse audiences, considering variations in literacy levels and communication preferences.

By integrating gender and diversity-specific indicators, stakeholder feedback mechanisms, periodic reviews, adaptive management strategies, and transparent reporting, the framework becomes a dynamic tool for ensuring the sustained success of the Gender Action Plan within the Improving Climate Resilience by Increasing Water Security project. The integration of gender and social inclusivity is not only a priority but a continually evolving and improving aspect of the project's impact on the community.
